4.7   Differentiating the trace and the weighted tensor drift
The next lemma records precisely the hypotheses on the continuation right sides that enter the
floor argument. They concern values and first derivatives only. Let E be a fixed compact set
containing the support of tr K and of all collar corrections. Fix 0 < δ < 1 and 3/4 < γ < 1, and
positive smooth weights ρ0 , ρ1 with

                                      ρ0 ≍ r−3−δ ,         ρ1 ≍ r−2γ

on the Euclidean end. Suppose

                                    |K| ≤ Cr−2 ,          |∇K| ≤ Cr−3

there. All constants defining E, the weights, and these bounds are fixed before N .
   For clarity denote the trace right side as a function of its inputs by F(x, w, h, p), and its value
on the current fields by F . The allowed forms are

   F1 = h + C(x),
 F2,θ = h + C(x) + θD0 (x, w, h),        0 ≤ θ ≤ 1,
 F3,ζ = h + (1 − ζ){C(x) + D0 (x, w, h + ζb(x))} + ζ{trAχ K + D1 (x, w)},          0 ≤ ζ ≤ 1. (4.44)


                                                     46
Here C, b are fixed smooth functions, and D0 , D1 are smooth bundle functions, supported over
E, that extend smoothly to |w| ≤ 1. Assume ∂h D0 ≥ 0. On the bounded height interval under
consideration, their values and first derivatives in the base, fiber, and height variables are bounded
independently of N . Only Aχ , through p, has N -dependent coefficients. Base derivatives of
bundle functions mean covariant derivatives with their fiber arguments parallel transported.
Lemma 4.14 (Floor derivative estimates). Assume the preceding hypotheses, h = τ f , and
|h| ≤ C0 . At any point where dt = 0, for each fixed η > 0 there are constants Cη and an integer
m, independent of N , the homotopy parameters, and the graph gradient, such that

                              w(F ) ≥ τ a| df | − ηT − Cη (1 + N )m (ρ0 + vρ1 ),                (4.45)
                    div(uAχ K(w, ·))
                                     ≤ ηT + Cη (ρ0 + vρ1 ).                                     (4.46)
                          u
If in addition |h| ≤ Cr−γ on the end, then

                                      |K|2 + vF 2 ≤ C(ρ0 + vρ1 ).                               (4.47)

The constants may depend on the fixed data, weights, compact corrections, height bound, and η;
they are uniform over the three stages in (4.44).
Proof. The error weights. Since δ + 2γ < 3, the end decay implies
                                        √
                              |∇K| ≤ C ρ0 ρ1 ,      |K|2 ≤ Cρ0 .

Young’s Inequality therefore gives

                                         a|∇K| ≤ C(ρ0 + vρ1 ).                                  (4.48)

Any bounded error supported in E is bounded by Cρ0 , because ρ0 has a positive minimum there.
   The exact tensor-drift derivative. Put H = H f , k0 = Kee , m0 = Ke⊥ , and PH = H|e⊥ . At
the point in question,

                          ∇p = 0,        ∇ log u = H(w, ·),      ∇w = HAd .

For c = (p + 4)/(4 + pv), Equation (4.31) reads

                                   Aχ K(w, ·) = K(w, ·) − cK(w, w)w.

Its exact differentiated form is
   div(uAχ K(w, ·))
                    = a{(div K)(e) − (1 − χ)(∇e K)(e, e)}
         u
                      + χ(2χ − 1)Hee k0 + 2χ⟨He⊥ , m0 ⟩ + PH : K⊥ − (1 − χ)(tr PH )k0 .         (4.49)

To see the cancellation explicitly, use a normal orthonormal frame at the point. The entries of
∇w = HAd are

                ∇e we = dHee ,      ∇e wA = HeA ,    ∇A we = dHAe ,    ∇A wB = HAB .

The coefficient of Hee k0 , after adding ⟨∇ log u, Aχ K(w, ·)⟩, is

                              d − 3cvd − 2(∂v c)v 2 d + vχ = χ(2χ − 1).

                                                    47
The mixed coefficient is 1 + d − 2cv + v = 2χ. The remaining transverse terms are the last two
terms of (4.49); differentiating K produces its first line. This proves the formula.
   In particular every axial Hessian term in this divergence contains a factor χ. Since H = S − K
and tr P = F − χq1 , the second line of (4.49) has absolute value at most

                    C|K| |P tf | + |M | + |F | + χ|q1 | + C|K|2 ≤ ηT + Cη |K|2 .
                                                         

             √
Here χ|q1 | ≤ χ|q1 | and (4.38) were used. The first line is bounded by Ca|∇K|. Equation (4.48)
proves (4.46), without an N -dependent constant.
    The trace derivative in each stage. At fixed (x, w, p), the height derivatives in (4.44) are
respectively
                    1,     1 + θ ∂ h D0 ,  1 + (1 − ζ)∂h D0 (x, w, h + ζb(x)),
so each is at least one. At dt = 0, the chain rule and h = τ f give

       w(F ) = ∂h F τ a| df | + (∇x F)(w) + Dw F(∇w w),             ∇w w = a(dHee e + He⊥ ).   (4.50)

There is no ∂p F term because ∇p = 0.
   The compact corrections have uniformly bounded first derivatives. For the only noncompact
correction, use
                     trAχ K = tr K − cK(w, w),   Dw c[ξ] = 2(∂v c)⟨w, ξ⟩.
The bounds
                             |c| ≤ C(1 + N ),            |∂v c| ≤ C(1 + N )2
give, on the stated height interval,

              |∇x F| ≤ C(1 + N )2 1E + |∇K| ,                |Dw F| ≤ C(1 + N )2 1E + |K| .
                                                                                        


The base derivative here also includes the bounded derivative of b(x) inside D0 . The second
term of (4.50) is consequently bounded below by −C(1 + N )2 (ρ0 + vρ1 ). For its third term, the
comparison (4.38) and d2 /χ ≤ (N + 4)/4 yield

                             C(1 + N )2 (1E + |K|) a |He⊥ | + d|Hee |
                                                                           

                                       ≤ ηT + Cη (1 + N )5 (1E + |K|2 ).

For example, replace H by S − K, use weighted Young Inequalities against |M |2 + χq12 , and
absorb the remaining K-terms into the same right side. Thus one may take m = 5, after enlarging
the constant. The weight observations at the start of the proof and ∂h F ≥ 1 establish (4.45).
   Finally, outside E, all three right sides have the form

                                          F = h + ζ trAχ K

with 0 ≤ ζ ≤ 1, where ζ = 0 in the first two stages. The eigenvalues of Aχ lie in (0, 1], so
|F | ≤ |h| + C|K| ≤ Cr−γ if the stated end height bound holds. On E, F is uniformly bounded
by the compact hypotheses. This proves (4.47).

Remark 4.15. The preceding propositions and lemmas are identities and estimates for smooth
fields. Their application to the continuation uses the right sides and compact corrections of
Proposition 4.17, the height bounds of Lemma 4.18, and the actual trace equation. In particular
no existence conclusion for the coupled system is part of the scalar-curvature calculation.

for the actual trace F ,
              Vν                                                                  √

Proof. The constant face value gives Hess f |T B = (∂ν f )IIg , and hence trT B H f = wν H. Using

                           u
                                               √                          √
This is the first identity. The normal in ḡ is d ν; its mean curvature is d H. The latter follows
also by subtracting the connection change from the tangential Hessian above. The conformal
boundary formula for ĝ = e4t ḡ yields the second identity. At df = 0 it follows by smooth
continuation, or by the same direct computation with d = 1.

4.8    A specified four-stage homotopy
Proposition 4.17 (The continuation family). There is a continuous piecewise smooth family,
with parameter ξ ∈ [0, 4], starting at Equations (4.18), (4.19), and (4.23), and ending at the zero
trace solution and scalar equation with zero source and inner flux. In the first three stages the
source is the Ξ just defined, always evaluated using the current trace F . Every trace right side
has derivative at least one in h, at fixed point, Z, and df . The outer values remain f = Z = 0.
Proof. Choose a smooth j : R → [0, 1], zero for t ≤ 0 and one for t ≥ 1. For 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1, define

                       Vλ = uAχ (4∇Z + λK(w, ·)),                     BK = Aχ K(w, ·).

Until the last scaling stage, the inner scalar condition is
                            (Vλ )ν
                                   = [1 − (1 − λ)j(t)][ BF − (1 − λ)(BK )ν ].                             (4.52)
                              u
    We specify the two fixed collar corrections. Choose η > 0 with 6η < b+ − b− . On black collars
take a directional cutoff χ− (w · νs ), supported where w · νs < −1/2 and equal to one at −1, and
a nonincreasing height cutoff η− (h), equal to one for h ≤ b− + η and zero for h ≥ b− + 2η. On
white collars use χ+ , supported where w · νs > 1/2 and equal to one at 1, and a nondecreasing
η+ , zero for h ≤ b+ − 2η and one for h ≥ b+ − η. Multiply by disjoint smooth collar cutoffs κB ,
equal to one on the faces, and set
                               X                                X
      D0 (x, w, h) = −A0                   κB χ− η − + A0                   κB χ+ η+ ,   A0 > 2 sup|H| + 1.
                           black collars                    white collars                        B

Thus ∂h D0 ≥ 0, D0 (x, 0, h) = 0; it is nonpositive at low heights h ≤ b− + η, nonnegative at high
heights h ≥ b+ − η, and zero in every compatible black outward or white inward direction, for all
lengths 0 ≤ |w| ≤ 1. All its derivatives on bounded height ranges are independent of N .
    At the assigned face height, the fully corrected expression F = h + C + D0 , evaluated at the
limiting normal directions w = ±ν, satisfies

                        F (x, +ν, b) ≥ PB + H,                 F (x, −ν, b) ≤ PB − H.                     (4.53)

                                                        49
These evaluations use |w| = 1 and χ = 0 in the smooth direction-free coefficients. Equation (4.12)
gives equality in the compatible direction. In the opposite direction the correction of magnitude
A0 gives the inequality.
    Take a smooth compactly supported extension b(x), constant at its assigned face value on
each smaller collar. Put
                                        X
                       D1 (x, w) = A1        κB (x) w · νs ,      A1 > sup|H| + 1.
                                         B                               B


The collars are disjoint. Thus D1 (x, 0) = 0 and D1 (x, ±ν) = ±A1 on every face. The family is as
follows.
   (i) For 0 ≤ ξ ≤ 1, put λ = 1 − ξ, retain F = h + C and h|B = b, and use div Vλ = uΞ with
       Equation (4.52).
  (ii) For 1 ≤ ξ ≤ 2, put λ = 0, α = ξ − 1, retain h|B = b, and set F = h + C + αD0 (x, w, h).
       Keep the same scalar equation and boundary condition.
 (iii) For 2 ≤ ξ ≤ 3, put λ = 0, ζ = ξ − 2, prescribe h|B = (1 − ζ)b, and set
                                                                                         
                   F = h + (1 − ζ) C + D0 (x, w, h + ζb(x)) + ζ trAχ K + D1 (x, w) .             (4.54)

      Keep the same scalar equation and boundary condition.
 (iv) For 3 ≤ ξ ≤ 4, retain this trace equation at ζ = 1, and multiply both Ξ and the right side
      of Equation (4.52) by sξ = 4 − ξ.
The prescriptions agree at their endpoints. The height derivatives are 1, 1 + α∂h D0 , and
1 + (1 − ζ)∂h D0 , respectively. At a stage-(iii) face, h + ζb = b, so its limiting directional value is
the convex combination of the earlier corrected value and PB + D1 (±ν). Equation (4.53) therefore
persists.
    At ζ = 1, the trace equation becomes trAχ H f = h + D1 (x, w), with zero boundary values. At
a positive interior maximum of f , w = 0, Aχ = I, and D1 = 0, so l∆f = τ f > 0, a contradiction.
A negative minimum is excluded similarly. Hence f = 0 and t = Z throughout the last stage.
At ξ = 4 the scalar equation is div(4L(Z)∇Z) = 0, with zero inner flux and zero outer Dirichlet
value. Multiplication by Z gives Z = 0. For a frozen-coefficient scalar solve at this endpoint, the
output is also identically zero for every input Z.

4.9    Uniform height bounds and the tracefree tail
Lemma 4.18 (Height and outer-boundary control). Every smooth trace solution in Proposi-
tion 4.17 satisfies |h| ≤ C0 , and its actual trace satisfies |F | ≤ CF , independently of N, R, ξ and
the input Z. There are fixed r0 , C > 0 and a lower bound Rht (N ) such that, when R ≥ Rht (N ),

              |h| ≤ C(r−γ − R−γ )       (r0 ≤ r ≤ R),          | df | ≤ Cτ −1 R−1−γ   on SR .    (4.55)

The constants in the bound are independent of N, R, ξ. For fixed N, ϵ, increasing the lower bound
on R ensures t > −ϵ at the outer boundary of every such solution.

Proof. The corrections D0 , D1 vanish at w = 0. At an interior extremum of f , the first two stages
give tr K + l∆f = h + C, and the third gives tr K + l∆f = h + (1 − ζ)C + ζ tr K. The maximum
and minimum inequalities bound h by ∥tr K∥∞ + ∥C∥∞ and the boundary heights. Boundedness
of the corrections and |trAχ K| ≤ 3|K| then bounds F . No floor has been used.


                                                     50
   Choose r0 outside the supports of C, D0 , D1 , b and tr K. The last stage has h = 0. In the
other stages the tail equation takes the form
                         l
                        √ trAχ Hess h − h + θ trAχ K = 0,             0 ≤ θ ≤ 1,               (4.56)
                       τ D
where θ = 1 in the first two stages and 1 − ζ in the third. Since tr K = 0,
                                                                     (p + 4)v
                         trAχ K = (χ − 1)K(e, e),        1−χ=                 .
                                                                      4 + pv

For the radial test ψ = r−γ , with its radial gradient axis, Equation (4.1) gives uniformly in
0<χ≤1
                 trAχ Hess ψ = γ (γ + 1)χ − 2 r−2−γ + O(r−3−γ ) ≤ −cγ r−2−γ
                                              

after increasing r0 .
    Consider ϕ = C(r−γ − R−γ ), choosing C large enough to dominate C0 at r = r0 for R ≥ 2r0 .
In the part r ≤ R/2, its height is at least C(1 − 2−γ )r−γ ; since γ < 1 < 2, this dominates the
bounded adverse tensor term O(r−2 ) after fixing r0 sufficiently large.
    In the part r ≥ R/2, evaluate coefficients at a possible comparison contact, so that the given
input Z and the test gradient agree with the solution’s. Put z = l| dϕ|/τ , noting | dϕ| ≍ Cr−1−γ .
If z ≥ 1, the magnitude of the negative Hessian term is at least cr−1 , whereas the adverse tensor

                                    CK (N + 4)(l/τ )2 C 2 r−4−2γ .

r0 , C have been fixed, makes ϕ a strict upper barrier. Its negative is a lower barrier by the same
absolute estimates. Height monotonicity allows comparison with the actual input Z. This proves
the first bound in Equation (4.55). Tangential derivatives vanish on SR ; comparison at their
common outer value gives the stated normal derivative bound.
     Finally, Z = 0 on SR , and the map t 7→ Z(t, df ) is increasing. Its value at t = −ϵ is negative
whenever
                                         ℓ2 | df |2 < e8ϵ − 1.
The boundary slope bound makes this true once

                                    C 2 ℓ2 τ −2 R−2−2γ < e8ϵ − 1.

This is a permissible further lower bound on R at fixed N, ϵ.

4.10    Exclusion of every floor contact
We now fix the constants in the source. Only estimates at a proposed floor contact are needed;
no upper bound on Z or on | df | is assumed.

Lemma 4.19 (Error ledger at an interior floor contact). There exist c0 > 0 and polynomial
constants CN such that every smooth first-three-stage solution with t ≥ −ϵ, at an interior point
with t = −ϵ, satisfies
                          u−1 div Vλ ≥ c0 T + τ aσ − CN (ρ0 + vρ1 ).                      (4.57)
Here c0 can be chosen independently of N, R, ξ.

                                                 51
Proof. At the contact dt = 0, Hess t ≥ 0, p = N , and dp = 0. Equation (4.32) and Lemma 4.12
give
           div V
                 ≥ 8πµ − 12 Rg + 8πJ(w) + v Ric(e, e) + T − S(H f ) + w(F ) − F tr K.
             u

                                 8πµ − 12 Rg = 21 (tr K)2 − |K|2 .
                                                                  

                       1

current trace and the omitted flux, from Lemma 4.14, retain τ aσ and each cost an arbitrarily
small fixed fraction of T . They have polynomial remainders.
   For clarity, every coefficient remainder has the following global control, with constants
independent of N, R, ξ:

           |K|2 ≤ Cρ0 ,                     vF 2 ≤ Cvρ1 ,                 |F tr K| ≤ Cρ0 ,
         v|Ric| ≤ Cvρ1 ,          a(|∇K| + |J|) ≤ C(ρ0 + vρ1 ).

    Choose the two small derivative fractions so that their sum is less than 1/32, and subtract
(1 − λ) div(uAχ K(w, ·))/u. This proves Equation (4.57), after renaming the positive universal
coefficient. No fixed-N regularity constant enters this ledger.

Proposition 4.20 (Floor separation along the full homotopy). Fix 0 < δ0 < min{c0 /2, 1/4},
where c0 is from Lemma 4.19. There is a polynomial choice of ΠN such that, for all sufficiently
large N , followed by all R ≥ Rfloor (N, ϵ), no smooth solution of Proposition 4.17 satisfies t ≥ −ϵ
and attains t = −ϵ. Every such solution has t > −ϵ on the closed truncation.
Proof. Enlarge Rfloor to include the outer-boundary requirement of Lemma 4.18. Thus an outer
contact is impossible.
   At an inner contact in one of the first three stages, j(t) = 0. Equation (4.52) cancels the
omitted drift on both sides and gives Vν /u = BF . Lemma 4.16 implies

                                     ∂ν t = − 14 (NB + H) < 0.

This contradicts the nonnegative inward derivative at a boundary minimum.
   At an interior contact, Equation (4.57) has right side at least 2δ0 T + τ aσ − CN WN . Choose
the polynomial ΠN so large that

                                    (ΠN − CN )WN ≥ 2ρ + ρ0 .

This is possible since WN ≥ ρ0 and ρ/ρ0 is bounded. At the floor m0 = 1; comparison with the
prescribed source would then give

                   0 ≥ δ0 T + (1 − δ0 )τ aσ + (ΠN − CN )WN − ρ ≥ ρ + ρ0 > 0,

                                                52
a contradiction.
    In the last stage f = 0, t = Z, u = L, and v = 0. At an interior floor minimum, T =
1     2 + (tr K)2 ). Enlarge the same polynomial so that δ T + ρ − Π ρ < 0 at every such point.
2 (|K|                                                     0           N 0
For sξ > 0, the scalar equation then has left side 4L∆t ≥ 0 and strictly negative right side. At an
inner floor minimum its boundary condition gives 4∂ν t = sξ (−H − NB ) < 0. Both are impossible.
If sξ = 0, multiplication of div(4L(t)∇t) = 0 by t, with zero outer value and zero inner flux, gives
t = 0.

   The estimates in this section concern every smooth candidate solution and its homotopy,
and thus supply the boundary exclusion needed for continuation. Existence is established in
Proposition 6.12, using the global estimates and regularity arguments of the following sections.


5     Local regularity for the rank-one trace equation
The estimate needed for the scalar trace equation is stronger than a general measurable-coefficient
nondivergence estimate. Its distinguished eigendirection is the gradient of the solution. We prove
the resulting gradient estimate first, and then use its Hessian measure bound in the second scalar
equation. All the estimates in this section are local in dimension three.
Theorem 5.1 (Rank-one gradient and Hessian estimates). Let U be an interior coordinate ball
or a smooth boundary half-ball, with a uniformly controlled C 2 metric g. In the boundary case
use Gaussian coordinates, so g33 = 1 and ga3 = 0 for a = 1, 2. Suppose that z is smooth on U , up
to the face on its original side in the boundary case, and satisfies
                                                                     ∇z
           Aij ∇i ∇j z = G,      Aij = g ij − (1 − b)ei ej ,   e=        ,     c0 ≤ b ≤ 1,       (5.1)
                                                                    |∇z|
where c0 > 0. In the boundary case assume that z is constant on the face. At a zero of ∇z, an
arbitrary measurable unit direction is allowed, provided Equation (5.1) holds with that choice.
Assume |∇z| ≤ L and |G| ≤ Q. There are α ∈ (0, 1), a uniform positive radius r0 , and C,
depending only on c0 , the indicated geometry, and separation from the other faces of the patch,
such that on a smaller patch
                                ˆ
    ∥Dz∥C 0,α ≤ C(L + Q),                | Hess z|2 dVg ≤ C(L + Q)2 r1+2α (0 < r ≤ r0 ).   (5.2)
                                   Br (x)∩U

The constants α, r0 , C do not depend on a modulus of continuity of b or G. The same assertion
holds for uniformly controlled families of patches.
    Here and below, smoothness can be replaced by the piecewise smooth regularity produced by
the reflection described in the proof. In particular, the estimates do not assume a uniform bound
for the Hessian.

5.1    A cutoff estimate and the gradient flux identity
Lemma 5.2 (A uniform exponent above two). There is p0 ∈ (2, 3) depending only on c0 with
the following property. If g is sufficiently close to the Euclidean metric in L∞ , with bounded
first derivatives, then the operator in Equation (5.1), with its coefficients held fixed, satisfies the
interior estimate
                       ∥v∥W 2,p0 (B1/2 ) ≤ C ∥v∥Lp0 (B1 ) + ∥Aij ∇i ∇j v∥Lp0 (B1 ) .
                                                                                  
                                                                                                  (5.3)

                                                   53
It also satisfies the analogous estimate with exponent two. The coefficient direction in this assertion
need not be the gradient direction of v.

Proof. For the full Hessian array the Fourier multiplier D2 ∆−1 has L2 operator norm one, since
       2 2      4                 p
  i,j ξi ξj /|ξ| = 1. Its finite L bounds and interpolation give constants Cp with Cp −→ 1 as
P

p ↓ 2. In Euclidean coordinates the Frobenius norm of I − A is exactly 1 − b. A sufficiently
small perturbation of the metric therefore gives |I − A| ≤ 1 − c0 /2. Choose p0 ∈ (2, 3) so that
Cp0 (1 − c0 /2) < 1. For a compactly supported v,

                       ∥D2 v∥p0 ≤ Cp0 ∥Aij Dij v∥p0 + Cp0 (1 − c0 /2)∥D2 v∥p0 .

Absorb the last term. Christoffel symbols add a bounded first-order term. Applying this inequality
to cutoffs on nested balls and using ∥Dv∥p ≤ ε∥D2 v∥p + Cε ∥v∥p gives Equation (5.3); the usual
nested-radius absorption removes the outer Hessian term. The same argument at p = 2 proves
the last assertion. All coefficient comparisons are pointwise, so no derivatives of b or of its axis
have been used.

    Put P = ∇z, H = Hess z, and w = |P |2 /2. A cancellation specific to the rank-one matrix
gives

                                            div A∇w − GP = |H|2 − (∆z)2 + Ric(P, P ).
                                                             
         A∇w − GP = (H − g∆z)P,                                                                   (5.4)

Indeed, ∇w = HP and G = ∆z − (1 − b)H(e, e); thus the terms containing 1 − b cancel
before differentiating. At P = 0 both cancelled vectors vanish. The second identity follows by
differentiating the remaining tensor expression and commuting the covariant derivatives of a
scalar. In particular it is meaningful even when b is measurable. Since

                                      ∆z = G + (1 − b)H(e, e),

choose a > 0 so that (1 + a)(1 − c0 )2 < 1. Young’s Inequality gives

                                   |H|2 − (∆z)2 ≥ c|H|2 − CG2 .                                   (5.5)

For c0 = 1 this follows directly from ∆z = G.
   We specify how Equations (5.4) and (5.5) are used at a boundary. Subtract the constant
boundary value, extend z oddly across x3 = 0, extend gab and b evenly, and extend G oddly.
Keep g33 = 1 and ga3 = 0. The extension of z is C 1 : its tangential derivatives vanish on the face
and its normal derivative extends evenly. Hence its distributional Hessian has no surface atom.
The doubled metric is Lipschitz and smooth on each side; we do not assert that its Ricci tensor is
a bounded function across the joining plane.
   Instead apply Equation (5.4) separately on the two sides. If J = (H − g∆z)P , its one-sided
normal component on the face is

                                   Jν = −zν trT ∂U H = ±H∂U zν2 .                                 (5.6)

The sign depends on the convention for the second fundamental form; only the bound |Jν | ≤
|H∂U | |P |2 is used. Consequently the jump of the flux is a plane density bounded by 2|H∂U | |P |2 .
A bounded density q(x′ )δ{x3 =d} is the divergence of the                        ′
                                                         √ bounded vector q(x )1{x3 >d} ∂3 . No
tangential derivative of q is required. Multiplication by det g converts the identity to Euclidean
divergence form.

                                                  54
    At any chosen center, a constant linear coordinate change makes g(0) = I. In Gaussian charts
it can be taken block diagonal, so a reflected plane remains a plane, possibly displaced from the
center. After a spatial dilation of size r, the deviations of g and its first derivatives from the
flat metric are O(r), the ordinary Ricci terms on each side are O(r2 ), and the reflected second
fundamental form is O(r). Normalize the dependent variable so |∇z| ≤ 1. If these quantities and
∥G∥∞ are at most ε, then s = 1 − |∇z|2 ≥ 0 satisfies

      −∂i (Aij ∂j s) ≥ c| Hess z|2 − Cε + div Eε ,                               Aij =
                                                                              det g Aij .
                                                                                         p
                                                           ∥Eε ∥∞ ≤ Cε,                     (5.7)
                                                                             √
The divergence here is the coordinate divergence. The vector Eε contains 2 det g GP and the
bounded vector representing the plane density. The bulk Ricci and G2 terms have been included
in Cε. This proves Equation (5.7) both in an interior ball and in a reflected ball, with uniform
ellipticity of A.

5.2    The fixed-axis equation and improvement of flatness
Lemma 5.3 (Regularity with a fixed axis). Let a be a fixed Euclidean unit vector and let
c0 ≤ b(x) ≤ 1 be measurable. If Y ∈ W 2,2 (B3/4 ) satisfies

                                             ∆a⊥ Y + bYaa = 0,                                (5.8)

then for some α1 ∈ (0, 1) depending only on c0 ,

                                  ∥Y ∥C 1,α1 (B1/4 ) ≤ C∥Y ∥W 2,2 (B3/4 ) .                   (5.9)

Proof. Rotate so that a = ∂3 and put v = Y3 . Differentiating Equation (5.8) in the sense of
distributions gives
                                ∂12 v + ∂22 v + ∂3 (b∂3 v) = 0.
This is a scalar uniformly elliptic divergence equation, even when b depends on all three variables.
The scalar De Giorgi–Nash estimate gives a C 0,α1 bound for v on a smaller ball, controlled by
∥v∥2 . Caccioppoli’s Inequality, applied after subtracting v(x), then gives for balls contained in
that smaller region             ˆ
                                          |Dv|2 ≤ Cr1+2α1 ∥Y ∥2W 2,2 (B3/4 ) .               (5.10)
                                 Br (x)

Now ∆Y = F = (1 − b)v3 . By Cauchy–Schwarz,
                            ˆ
                                 |F | ≤ Cr2+α1 ∥Y ∥W 2,2 (B3/4 ) .                           (5.11)
                                   Br (x)

Take a cutoff equal to one on B1/3 , supported where these estimates hold, and let V be the
Newton potential of the cut-off F . Its gradient kernel is bounded by C|x|−2 and the derivative
of that kernel by C|x|−3 . If d = |x − y|, the contribution to |DV (x) − DV (y)| from distances at
most 2d is bounded, by dyadic annuli and Equation (5.11), by C 2−j ≤2d (2−j )α1 ≤ Cdα1 . At
                                                                     P

larger distances the bound is Cd 2−j >d (2−j )α1 −1 ≤ Cdα1 . The same bounds hold for the cut-off
                                   P

source, with an additional harmless large-scale term. Thus DV is C 0,α1 . The Newton kernel is
locally in L2 in dimension three, so ∥V ∥∞ is controlled by ∥F ∥2 . Finally Y − V is harmonic on
B1/3 ; its ordinary interior derivative estimates complete Equation (5.9).

Lemma 5.4 (Drop or affine approximation). Fix r∗ ∈ (0, 1/32]. For every h∗ > 0 there are

                                              sup |∇z| ≤ k∗ ,                                   (5.12)
                                              Br∗

                                                           1
                               sup |z − L| ≤ h∗ r∗ ,       2 ≤ |DL| ≤ 2.                        (5.13)
                               Br∗

If the center lies on the reflecting plane and z has zero boundary value, L can be chosen odd about
that plane.
Proof. Otherwise take errors εj → 0 and kj ↑ 1 with neither alternative, and subtract zj (0) in
interior balls. Lemma 5.2, together with the gradient bound, bounds zj in W 2,p0 on smaller balls.
Thus Dsj is bounded in Lp0 . Failure of Equation (5.12) gives inf B1/6 sj → 0. The weak Harnack
Inequality with bounded divergence and scalar errors implies sj → 0 in measure on B1/3 ; one
may use Theorem 5.2 and Corollary 5.4 of [30], with the radii 1/6, 1/3, and 5/6.
    We need more than this convergence in measure. Test Equation (5.7), after dropping its
nonnegative Hessian term, with η 2 (h − sj )+ , where η is supported in B1/3 . Ellipticity and Young’s
Inequality give, for fixed h > 0,
                             ˆ                          ˆ
                                      2        2      2
                                     η |Dsj | ≤ Ch         |Dη|2 + oj (1).                       (5.14)
                               {sj <h}

On {sj ≥ h}, convergence in measure and the uniform L2 bound for Dsj give convergence to zero
of its L1 norm. On the complementary set, Equation (5.14) and Cauchy–Schwarz bound the L1
norm by Ch + oj (1). First let j → ∞, then h ↓ 0. We obtain Dsj → 0 in L1 (B1/4 ). Testing the
full Equation (5.7) with a nonnegative cutoff now yields
                                     ˆ
                                          | Hess zj |2 −→ 0.
                                           B1/8

Since gj → I and Dgj → 0, the same is true for D2 zj . Poincaré’s Inequality and the uniform
Lipschitz bound show, after passage to a subsequence, uniform convergence on smaller balls to an
affine function. Its slope has length one, because sj → 0 in measure and the gradients converge
strongly in L2 . This contradicts failure of Equation (5.13). At a boundary center the functions
are odd, so their affine limit has zero constant and tangential terms. Increasing k∗ if necessary
ensures k∗ > r∗ .

5.3   A bounded scalar equation with natural growth
Lemma 5.6 (Hölder control from a Hessian measure bound). Suppose Z is locally Lipschitz,
|Z| ≤ M , and satisfies, in distributions on a ball in R3 ,
                   div(aDZ + B) = e,          λI ≤ a = aT ≤ ΛI,       ∥B∥∞ ≤ B0 ,              (5.19)
where a is measurable and e is a signed Radon measure. Assume
              |e| ≤ C0 (1 + |DZ|2 ) dx + µ,       µ(Br (x)) ≤ Cµ r1+β ,       0 < β ≤ 1,       (5.20)
for all smaller balls, where µ is nonnegative. Then Z has a local C 0,θ bound for some θ > 0,
depending only on the displayed constants and the patch separation. No bound for its local Lipschitz
constant enters this estimate. The conclusion also holds at a smooth boundary with bounded total
conormal flux, or with constant Dirichlet value.

                                                  58
Proof. Write L = − div(aD), choose k ≥ max{1, 2C0 /λ}, and set Vs = eskZ for s = ±1. The
exact distributional chain rule is

                   LVs = div(skVs B) − skVs e − k 2 Vs aDZ · DZ + B · DZ .
                                                                                   
                                                                                               (5.21)

It is valid for the present weak equation: the exponential is locally Lipschitz and can be
approximated uniformly and in W 1,2 in the test, so the measure term converges as well. The
inequality |B · DZ| ≤ (λ/2)|DZ|2 + B02 /(2λ) and Equation (5.20) imply
                                                                            !
                                                       kM         k 2 B02
      LVs ≤ div Fs + ν,      Fs = skVs B,       ν=e         kC0 +               dx + kekM µ.   (5.22)
                                                                    2λ

In particular Fs is bounded and ν is a positive measure with the same admissible growth, in
addition to a bounded density.
   The correction estimate. We record the small correction estimate on BR :

                Lws = div Fs + ν,       ws ∈ W01,2 (BR ),     ∥ws ∥∞ ≤ εR = CRβ .              (5.23)

For the bounded vector term, testing the Dirichlet equation with (w − t)+ gives, for p > 3,
                          ˆ
                             |D(w − t)+ |2 ≤ C∥Fs ∥2p |{w > t}|1−2/p .

Sobolev’s Inequality then yields

                    |{w > h}| ≤ C∥Fs ∥6p (h − t)−6 |{w > t}|3−6/p ,         h > t.

The exponent on the right is greater than one. Iteration, also for −w, gives ∥w∥∞ ≤
CR1−3/p ∥Fs ∥p ≤ CR.
   For the measure term the positive scalar Dirichlet Green function satisfies GBR (x, y) ≤
C|x − y|−1 . This is the scalar measurable-coefficient Green bound: extend a elliptically to R3 ,
use the fundamental-solution bound in Theorem 3.1 of [14], and compare the Dirichlet Green
function by the weak maximum principle. The scalar De Giorgi–Nash estimate supplies the local
Hölder hypothesis of that theorem. Dyadic annuli and Equation (5.20) give
                                 ˆ
                             sup    GBR (x, y) dν(y) ≤ C(R2 + Rβ ).
                             x     BR

This also constructs the needed weak correction without assuming measure solvability. Approxi-
mate the restricted positive measure by smooth positive measures, preserving the ball-growth
bound. The resulting potentials have the displayed uniform supremum bound, and their energy is
bounded by their supremum times the total mass. A weak W01,2 limit solves the measure equation
and retains the bound. Adding the vector and measure corrections proves Equation (5.23).
   Oscillation decay. Let MR = supBR Z, mR = inf BR Z, and set

              H+ = ekMR − ekZ + w+ + εR ,            H− = e−kmR − e−kZ + w− + εR .             (5.24)

These functions are nonnegative weak supersolutions of LH± ≥ 0. Each exponential deficit is
bounded above and below by positive constants, depending on kM , times MR − Z or Z − mR ,
respectively. One of the two midpoint sets

                {Z ≤ (MR + mR )/2} ∩ BR/3 ,           {Z ≥ (MR + mR )/2} ∩ BR/3

                                                59
has at least half the volume of BR/3 . Apply weak Harnack to the corresponding H± . In
Theorem 5.2 of [30], take the inner radius R/6: its averaging ball is BR/3 and its required
nonnegativity ball is B5R/6 . Thus the infimum on BR/6 is at least c(MR − mR ). Removing
the correction in Equation (5.24) costs at most 2εR . It follows that either the upper endpoint
decreases or the lower endpoint increases by c(MR − mR ) − CεR , whence

                                oscBR/6 Z ≤ (1 − c) oscBR Z + CRβ .                           (5.25)

Iteration gives any sufficiently small positive exponent below both β and − log(1 − c)/ log 6.
    Boundary reflection. Flatten a boundary face and put S = diag(1, 1, −1). For an even
extension of Z, extend a− = Sa+ S and B − = SB + . If q is the third component of the original
total flux on x3 = 0, the reflected equation has the additional density 2q δ{x3 =0} . It is bounded
by the prescribed total conormal bound, after the coordinate volume factors are included. For
a constant Dirichlet value, extend Z minus that value oddly and use a− = Sa+ S, B − = −SB +
and the odd bulk source. The normal flux is then continuous, with no plane term. The reflected
matrix remains measurable and uniformly elliptic. A bounded plane density has ball mass O(r2 ),
which is allowed in Equation (5.20) because β ≤ 1. This proves the boundary assertion.

5.4   Classical bootstrap from bounded scalar variables
Proposition 5.7 (The regularity interface for the coupled system). Consider a family of smooth
solutions (f, Z) on smooth finite domains with uniformly controlled interior and boundary patches.
Assume |f | + |Df | + |Z| ≤ M and the following structural bounds, uniformly in the family:
  (i) The f -equation has the form of Equation (5.1), with b ≥ c0 > 0 and bounded G. Its actual
      matrix and source are smooth functions of (x, Z, f, Df ) on the bounded variable range,
      including at Df = 0. The value of f on each boundary face is constant.
 (ii) After multiplication by the coordinate volume density, the Z-equation is

        ∂i aij (x, Z, f, Df )∂j Z + B i (x, Z, f, Df ) = Q,   |Q| ≤ C(1 + |DZ|2 + |D2 f |2 ), (5.26)
                                                   


      where a is symmetric and uniformly elliptic and B is bounded. The functions a, B and Q,
      as a function also of (DZ, D2 f ), are smooth on the ranges on which they are used.
(iii) Each boundary component for Z has either constant Dirichlet data, or bounded total conormal
      flux. In the latter case its equation can also be solved as

                                           ∂ν Z = H(x, Z, f, Df ),                            (5.27)

      where H is smooth on the bounded variable range. These faces are disjoint and have no
      edges where the type of data changes.
Assume that the derivatives of the displayed smooth functions and the geometry are bounded to
the order needed for each stated estimate. Then f and Z have uniform local classical estimates of
every such finite order, including at the boundary. The constants are independent of the size of a
finite exhaustion domain if the patch geometry and all the displayed data bounds are independent
of that size.
Proof. Theorem 5.1 gives a uniform C 1,α bound for f and
                                  ˆ
                                         |D2 f |2 ≤ Cr1+2α .                                  (5.28)
                                        Br (x)


                                                   60
Coordinate connection terms do not affect this estimate. Apply Lemma 5.6 to Equation (5.26)
with µ = C|D2 f |2 dx and β = min{2α, 1}. At a flux boundary include the bounded reflected
plane density. We obtain a uniform Hölder bound for Z. Smooth composition in the bounded
variables now makes the leading matrix and source of the f -equation Hölder. The interior and
constant-Dirichlet Schauder estimates give f ∈ C 2,θ for some uniform θ > 0.
    Expand Equation (5.26) in nondivergence form. Its leading matrix is uniformly elliptic and
C 0,θ . Differentiating its coefficients produces terms at most quadratic in DZ, because D2 f is now
bounded. Thus its right-hand side is bounded by C(1 + |DZ|2 ). Fix p > 3. Linear local W 2,p
estimates, with either constant Dirichlet or the normal data in Equation (5.27), give on nested
patches Ux ⋐ Vx ⋐ Vx+
                                                                            
                       ∥Z∥W 2,p (Ux ) ≤ C 1 + ∥DZ∥2L2p (Vx ) + ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx+ ) .               (5.29)

The patch size here is chosen from the already established Hölder modulus of the leading matrix.
The normal boundary operator is uniformly oblique; the local a priori estimate is, for example, a
smooth-boundary specialization of Theorem 2.3 of [13], followed by localization. Only its estimate
is used, not solvability for a pure oblique problem. The boundary norm is controlled by
                                                                             
                      ∥H(x, Z, f, Df )∥W 1−1/p,p (∂Vx ) ≤ C 1 + ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx+ ) .              (5.30)

Indeed, extend the smooth expression for H through the collar, differentiate it there, use the
already bounded first two derivatives of f , and apply the trace theorem. This explains the last
term in Equation (5.29) without assuming a gradient bound for Z.
    We give the absorption of the quadratic gradient term. On balls or half-balls of radius h, the
scaled interpolation estimate is

                 ∥DZ∥2L2p (Bh ) ≤ C oscB2h Z ∥D2 Z∥Lp (B2h ) + Ch3/p−2 (oscB2h Z)2 .           (5.31)
                                                         ´
One way to obtain it is to subtract a constant, integrate η 2p |DZ|2p by parts against Z, and use
Hölder and Young Inequalities on the terms containing D2 Z and Dη. At the boundary use a
bounded extension operator on a half-ball, bounded also in L∞ ; its lower derivative terms are
absorbed by the same interpolation. Scaling gives exactly the second power of h displayed in
Equation (5.31).
    Write [Z]C 0,θ ≤ H0 . Cover Vx by radius-h patches of bounded overlap, with their doubles in
Vx . Take the ℓp sum of Equation (5.31). There are O(h−3 ) patches, so
  +


                        ∥DZ∥2L2p (Vx ) ≤ CH0 hθ ∥D2 Z∥Lp (Vx+ ) + CH02 h2θ−2 .                 (5.32)

Also, for every η > 0, ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx+ ) ≤ η∥D2 Z∥Lp (Vx++ ) + Cη on a further uniformly enlarged patch.
Such enlarged patches are covered by a bounded number of the Uy . On a fixed smooth finite-
domain solution let T = supy ∥Z∥W 2,p (Uy ) , which is finite before making any uniform assertion.
Equations (5.29) and (5.32) imply

                                   T ≤ C(H0 hθ + η)T + C(h, η).                                (5.33)

The radius for the linear estimate was fixed first. Now choose the auxiliary covering radius h,
and then η, so that the coefficient of T is less than 1/2. This proves a uniform W 2,p bound, and
hence Z ∈ C 1,1−3/p .


                                                  61
    The source in the nondivergence Z-equation is now Hölder; the normal data have the cor-
                                                                          ′
responding first derivative regularity. Schauder estimates give Z ∈ C 2,θ . The f -equation then
gains another derivative. Repeating this argument gives every finite order supported by the data.
All coverings and constants were uniform, so this conclusion has the asserted independence from
the exhaustion radius.

Remark 5.8. The hypotheses in Proposition 5.7 are an interface to be checked from the actual
deformation equations. In particular the lower bound for b, the bounded variables, the quadratic
source estimate, and the solvable normal boundary condition are required before applying the
proposition. The argument supplies classical bounds on fixed-size patches; it does not by itself give
decay at infinity. Uniform estimates of arbitrarily high order on an end require correspondingly
high derivative bounds for the reduced background data.


6     Existence of the flat deformation and its mass comparison
We retain the prepared exterior Ω, the inner faces B = Bb ⊔ Bw , and the four-stage family of
Proposition 4.17. The parameter of that family is ξ ∈ [0, 4], with ξ = 0 the physical system.
All the geometric choices in Lemma 4.8 are fixed throughout this section. A finite truncation
is denoted by ΩR . The normal ν on B points into ΩR ; its negative is the normal used in the
divergence theorem. We write

                        σ = |df |g ,      e = ∇f /σ   (σ > 0),    |ζ|2Aj = Aj (ζ, ζ)

for a covector ζ. Expressions at σ = 0 are understood by their smooth extensions established in
Section 4.
    We first obtain collar and global bounds for arbitrary smooth solutions. We then construct a
solution by scalar solvability, degree, and exhaustion, and conclude with the curvature and mass
comparison.
    There are two distinct types of constants. A symbol PN denotes a quantity bounded by
C(1 + N )q , where C, q depend on the fixed data, collars, and ϵ, but not on N , R, or ξ. Such a
polynomial may be increased at successive occurrences. A constant CN may depend arbitrarily
on the fixed positive parameters ϵ, N, ℓ, τ , but remains independent of R and ξ. In particular,
                                                             4d                        τ
           ℓ = e−ϵN ,        τ = ℓ3/2 ,      ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,         ≤ χ ≤ d,         PN      −→ 0.   (6.1)
                                                            4+N                        ℓ
All estimates preceding the existence proof concern arbitrary smooth solutions with t ≥ −ϵ,
including a putative solution touching that level. No estimate for a Hessian or upper bound for
Z is assumed at this stage.

6.1   Closed sublevels and signed collar estimates
Write Bc for the full black trapped region at threshold c, and Wc for the corresponding white
region with Bcb held fixed. These regions, including their Hausdorff right continuity at the selected
thresholds, are those of Lemma 4.8.
Lemma 6.1 (Separation from the opposite height). In the first two stages, if a fixed compact
subset Q of the closed prepared exterior is disjoint from Bcb , there are ηQ > 0 and NQ such that

                                            h ≥ b− + ηQ    on Q

for N ≥ NQ and all sufficiently large allowed truncations. If Q is disjoint from Wcw , then instead

Proof. Consider any sequence Ni → ∞, Ri → ∞ of the indicated solutions. Lemma 4.18 gives,
with fixed constants,
                                                                            3

modulus is required. Let

                           h(x) = lim inf{hi (y) : i ≥ j, dist(x, y) < j −1 }.
                                   j→∞

This is a bounded lower semicontinuous function satisfying the limiting end bound.
    Choose b− < k ′ < 0 so near b− that the stage-two collar term is nonpositive at every height
at most k ′ . Its positive white part vanishes in this fixed low-height range. Suppose ϕ is a
smooth lower test of h at x, where h(x) ≤ k ′ and dϕ(x) ̸= 0. For now exclude the black faces.
Subtracting a small positive multiple of dist(x, ·)4 makes contact strict without altering the
two-jet. Minimization on a small closed ball supplies points xi → x and constants tending to zero
for which the translated tests touch hi from below and hi (xi ) → h(x). They cannot touch the
boundary of the small ball. They cannot touch a white face or its extended side either, because
hi = b+ > k ′ there. Thus these are interior contacts, even if x itself lies on a white face.

The left side is the expansion of the level set, oriented toward increasing ϕ. Removing the

   To pass from functions to all supports of a closed set, fix b− < k < k ′ and put Ek = {h ≤ k}.
Define                                                     −1
                     qj (s) = 1 + exp[−j 2 (s − k − j −1 )] ,     vj = qj (h).
The lower relaxed limit of vj is zero on Ek and one off it. At an equality point this follows from
qj (k) = (1 + ej )−1 → 0; off Ek , lower semicontinuity gives a neighborhood whose heights exceed
k by a fixed positive amount. Let ψ be a nonzero-gradient lower test of this limit at a zero value.
Strict localization gives lower tests of vj at points yj → x with vj (yj ) → 0. Their original heights
are below k ′ for large j, since qj (k ′ ) → 1. For each fixed such j the smooth inverse qj−1 turns this

                                                      63
into a lower test of h with finite nonzero gradient. Apply (6.3) to that inverse test, taking the
solution-sequence limit with j fixed, and only then let j → ∞. An increasing change of defining
function preserves the level expansion: the additional Hessian term is a multiple of dψ ⊗ dψ and
has zero tangential trace. Consequently every such test ψ satisfies (6.3).
    A smooth local exterior support of Ek has a defining function that is zero at contact and
nonpositive on Ek . Shrinking and scaling the neighborhood so its positive values are below one
makes it exactly a lower test of the preceding zero–one function. Thus every exterior support has
expansion at most k ′ + Cb . In particular Ek cannot touch a white face: the reversed face is an
exterior support with expansion

                                      −H + trT K = −cw > 0,

whereas k ′ + Cb < 0 after k ′ is chosen sufficiently close to b− . Equivalently, in a signed white
collar with s ≥ 0 in Ω, a small multiple of −s is the offending lower test. This rules out boundary
layers without assuming that the Dirichlet values persist in the relaxed limit. The end bound
makes Ek compact.
    Adjoin the entire black region and fill complementary components which contain no designated
outer barrier. An exterior support at a black frontier point also supports the smooth black region,
so its expansion is at most cb < k ′ + Cb . At other frontier points the preceding argument applies.
Filling produces no frontier point outside the old closed set; every support of the larger filled set
is still a support of that old set. The support bound is therefore preserved. The original required
inner collars are covered by the black region, and the outer barriers remain outside. Lemma 4.7
places the resulting set in Bc′′ for any sufficiently close threshold c′′ > k ′ + Cb .
    For a fixed compact Q disjoint from Bcb , right continuity gives δ > 0 with Bcb +δ ∩ Q = ∅.
Choose
                               b− < k < k ′ ,     k ′ + Cb < c′′ < cb + δ.
Then Ek ∩ Q = ∅ for every sequence considered above. Failure of a uniform lower margin would
give violating points in Q, a convergent subsequence, and a relaxed limit value at most k there.
This contradiction proves the lower assertion. Apply the argument to (−h, −K, −C) for the
upper assertion. In the white problem, filling retains all reversed black outer faces. If the white
region is empty, a nonempty violating sublevel would still provide the seed for Lemma 4.7; right
continuity at the empty region rules out such seeds at arbitrarily close higher thresholds. Multiple
components and bounded complementary pockets cause no change.
    For the truncation quantifier start with any Rmin (N ) → ∞ satisfying the end estimates, and
enlarge it as needed. Failure for arbitrarily large N and R ≥ Rmin (N ) would provide exactly
a sequence excluded above. Only finitely many fixed compact sets will be used below, so their
thresholds can be chosen simultaneously.

The directional inequalities in Proposition 4.17 give the initial residual signs. Formula (4.54)
evaluates its shifted height at the original face value, so these signs persist along the interpolation.
At the unshifted face height all errors are O(s + d); changing the height supplies a margin at least
the absolute height change. Large fixed slopes absorb the O(s) errors and order the outer edge,
and the same logarithmic correction handles the initial O(d) errors. This proves the absolute
upper slope bound without using separation in stage three. Finally combine the own-color lower
collar bounds with Lemma 6.1 on the rest of a fixed compact set. The opposite bound on each
collar follows from that lemma as well. A finite cover proves the asserted height range.

contributes 4uAχ : Hess Z ≤ 0. Equations (6.11) and (6.16) contradict the scalar equation. The
compact upper bound and outer value zero therefore give a global first-stage bound.
    In stages two and three the drift is zero and the source is positive above a fixed high level, so
an interior maximum there is impossible. At an inner face
                                    Z ≤ t + 18 log(1 + e2 C12 /τ 2 ).
Consequently high boundary Z implies t > 1, where the boundary multiplier makes the conormal
derivative zero. The boundary point principle on the high superlevel excludes that maximum.
These maximum principles apply to each smooth solution with its positive definite coefficients,
so they require no prior uniform ellipticity bound. Stage four has f = 0 and t = Z. Its positive
scales have the same high-level argument; at scale zero its homogeneous mixed problem gives
Z = 0. This is uniform as the scale tends to zero.
    Finally D = e8(Z−t) and l ≥ ℓ give
                           |df |2 = l−2 (e8(Z−t) − 1) ≤ ℓ−2 (e8(CN +ϵ) − 1).
The height bound was already proved. The finite set of compact separation and collar choices,
followed by the polynomial absorption, fixes N0 . Enlarge Rmin (N ) to contain all fixed patches
and the end thresholds. The preceding arguments apply to floor-contact solutions as well.

6.3     Classical compactness and the scalar solution operator
The analytic estimates in Section 5 are proved independently of the deformation. In particular
they do not assume a modulus of continuity for the axial coefficient.
Proposition 6.7 (Classical bounds). At fixed ϵ, N , the solutions in Proposition 6.6 have local
bounds of every finite classical order, up to the inner and outer faces, uniform in ξ and in all
sufficiently large R. On the end one can use patches with a fixed positive radius and uniform
constants.
Proof. On the bounded variable range l, u, D are bounded above and away from zero and
χ ≥ cN > 0. The normalized trace equation is

after subtracting a constant uses small Hölder oscillation of Z to absorb the quadratic gradient
term in local W 2,p estimates. The normal datum’s trace norm is controlled by a lower-order
W 1,p norm; the derivatives of f entering that datum are already bounded. Subsequent Schauder
estimates give every finite order. The data have uniform smooth end-patch bounds, and the
large outer spheres have uniform boundary-chart constants. The local supremum-of-patch-norm
argument in Proposition 5.7 therefore makes all these estimates independent of R.

    We first solve the trace equation for prescribed Z. This will define the compact map used
in the degree argument below, and the same scalar result will also apply to the later spatially
varying lapse.

                       f (x) − f (y) − ψ(dist(x, y))   for 0 < dist(x, y) < r0

                                         ′
one further differentiation gives C 3,b . The linearization has positive definite principal matrix
and negative zeroth-order coefficient −∂f Qs . The Dirichlet maximum principle and linear elliptic
theory give its inverse. The implicit function theorem supplies openness of continuation from
∆g f = c∗ f ; the uniform estimates and compactness supply closedness. Comparison at a maximum
of the difference proves uniqueness, because the gradients agree and the height source is strictly
increasing. The implicit function theorem, or compactness and uniqueness, gives the asserted
dependence and uniform bounds.

6.4   Degree, exhaustion, and end normalization
Lemma 6.10 (Degree on moving sections). Let X be a Banach space and H : [0, 1] × X → X
a continuous map taking bounded sets to relatively compact sets. Let O ⊂ [0, 1] × X be a
bounded relatively open set. If its closure has no fixed point on its relative product boundary, then
deg(I − Hs , Os , 0) is independent of s. Here Os = {x : (s, x) ∈ O}, and the degree of an empty
section is zero.
Proof. The fixed set in O is compact by the joint compactness and continuity, and it lies in O
by hypothesis. For each parameter, surround its compact fixed-point set by a bounded open
neighborhood whose closure is contained in all sufficiently nearby sections. All nearby fixed
points of O lie in this neighborhood: otherwise a sequence outside it would converge to a fixed
point at the original parameter outside the neighborhood. Excision identifies the section degree
with the degree on this one neighborhood, and ordinary homotopy invariance makes it constant
locally. At a parameter without fixed points the same compactness argument makes all nearby
degrees zero. A finite covering of the parameter interval proves the assertion. These are the
compact-perturbation degree and excision properties of Leray–Schauder [19].

Proposition 6.11 (Finite-domain existence). For each fixed sufficiently small ϵ > 0, all suffi-

Theorem 6.15 (Completion of the flat deformation estimate). The flat exterior of Theorem 4.1
satisfies                                  s
                                              A∗
                                      Eg ≥        .
                                              16π
Proof. For each fixed large N , the metric of Lemma 6.13 has a strictly mean-negative inner
boundary and strictly mean-positive large coordinate spheres. Apply the three-dimensional
trapped-region theorem with zero tensor to obtain its full outermost minimal enclosing frontier. It
is a compact smooth surface, possibly disconnected, and bounds the connected exterior containing
the end. It is outer area-minimizing: its minimizing enclosure exists by the direct method between
the fixed barriers; minimal-obstacle regularity and the maximum principle make that enclosure a
smooth minimal bounding frontier. A strict enlargement would contradict maximality of the full
minimal trapped region. These are also the outermost/outer-minimizing horizon conventions in
the Riemannian Penrose Theorem [5, 6].
    Let this full cut be ΓN . Its exterior is complete with boundary, has one AF end of order one
through two derivatives, and has nonnegative scalar curvature with pointwise falloff O(r−3−δ ).
These satisfy the boundary version of the Riemannian Penrose Theorem [6, Theorem 1.4]; no
extension across the original boundary is needed. Every cut in the prepared exterior is a full
enclosing cut of the original flat obstacle. The pointwise metric inequality and the two-dimensional
area scaling therefore give
                                     |ΓN |gb ≥ e−4ϵ |ΓN |g ≥ e−4ϵ A∗ .
Lemmas 6.13 and 6.14 imply
                                               s                      s
                                  εN               |ΓN |gb                A∗

                                  8π                16π                   16π
First the end was exhausted at fixed N . Now let N → ∞ with ϵ fixed, and then let ϵ ↓ 0. Only
numerical estimates are passed to the last two limits; no smooth limit of the comparison metrics
as N → ∞ is used. This completes Theorem 4.1.

7     Removal of the second fundamental form on an asymptotically
      hyperbolic end
All norms and differentiated estimates in this section are measured in the hyperbolic reference
metric on balls of fixed hyperbolic radius, unless another convention is stated. Thus Oj (r−a )
includes covariant derivatives through order j, without a loss of a power of r. The letter r is
extended to a positive smooth function on the compact part of the manifold. An enclosing cut
always means the entire intrinsic boundary of a connected exterior containing the designated end.

norm between g and qR . The noncompact terms are bounded by a coefficient tending to zero times
    ′
r−2τ . Since 2τ ′ > 3 + δ, these are o(1)w0 . The fixed-annulus terms have the same conclusion by
compact positivity of w0 . This argument uses no extra pointwise decay of the original density µ.

by the differentiated cutoff estimates above. Here and below ν at an inner boundary points into
the exterior. This is a scalar equation with a bounded first-order drift and a positive zeroth-order
coefficient. A complete continuation argument is as follows. On a finite truncation impose zero
outer values and multiply the right side and the inner normal derivative by a parameter a ∈ [0, 1].
Subtract aηR times a fixed collar function with normal derivative −1; the remaining function
has homogeneous Neumann data. At its extrema the gradient term is bounded by the fixed
collar derivatives and the regularizing source. The Neumann maximum principle therefore gives
                                             ′
a uniform O(ηR ) bound. On the end, r−τ is a supersolution for the equation with any of its
bounded decaying drifts, because 3 + 2τ ′ − (τ ′ )2 > 0. Hence
                                                          ′
                                          vR = O2 (ηR r−τ ).

The estimates of Lemma A.1, first absorbing the bounded first-order term by interpolation and
then applying the one-sided Schauder estimate, give closedness of continuation. Its linearization
has positive zeroth order and the same mixed boundary conditions, so the maximum principle
and linear elliptic theory give openness. Exhaustion now gives the displayed solution. On the
exact tail the sources decay faster than r−κ ; comparison improves the bound to that power. The
regularizer w2 bounds all differentiated coefficients on rescaled balls. Bootstrap gives the stated
estimates of all orders at fixed R.

   We include the mass argument because uniform metric convergence´alone would not prove it.
The original constraint and the weighted integrability assumption imply V0 |Rg +6| dVg < ∞. For
any static potential Va , its scalar-linearization flux has divergence Va DRb (g − b). The difference


                                                 79
between DRb (g − b) and Rg + 6 is quadratic in the metric error and its first two derivatives. Its
weighted radial integral is bounded by
                                       ˆ ∞
                                                ′
                                     C     r2−2τ dr < ∞.

Equation (7.8) makes the curvature tail uniformly small, and the quadratic error has the same
uniform integrable bound. Compare the fluxes on a fixed large sphere, where the data converge,
and then send that sphere to infinity. Since |Va | + |dVa | ≤ CV0 , this proves convergence of all
four components. It also proves their existence for each approximating metric. The improved tail
bounds and the constraints give all the weighted integrability requirements for the new data. If
the original data have only the stated finite regularity, the constructed data are already smooth
outside a compact set: there the vector equation is homogeneous on b, and all coefficients and
sources of the scalar equation are smooth after bootstrap. On the remaining compact set, smooth
the metric in C 2 and the tensor in C 1 , with a cutoff leaving that smooth tail unchanged. The strict
energy margin and strict boundary expansion have positive minima in absolute value on their
respective compact sets. Continuity of scalar curvature in C 2 , momentum divergence in C 1 , and
boundary expansion in these norms therefore preserves both inequalities if the smoothing error is
sufficiently small. Choose that error to tend to zero with R. This gives smooth approximants
with unchanged asymptotic estimates and mass components.
    Finally, uniform bilinear ratio convergence bounds every cut area between factors tending to
one, and therefore bounds their infima by the same factors.

   It suffices henceforth to prove Theorem 7.1 for fixed data satisfying Equation (7.3). All
constants from this preparation are fixed before any parameter of the following deformation is
chosen.

7.2   The compact domain, the lapse, and the profiles
Coordinate spheres have both expansions tending to 2. The maximum-radius comparison therefore
confines all bounding regions at negative thresholds near zero to a common compact set. Apply
Lemmas 4.2, 4.3, and 4.4 as follows. First take the full black region at a fixed right-continuity
threshold cb < 0, using the given boundary as required inner boundary. In its complement take
the full white region for −K, at a right-continuity threshold cw < 0; the reversed black faces are
strict outer barriers, and this second region may be empty. Retain the closed component toward
infinity after deleting both regions. Its boundary consists of entire black and white faces. Every
full cut in this smaller exterior is a full cut in the original one.
    The compact geometric lemmas apply without a cosmological or energy assumption: their
hypotheses are smooth compact geometry, the stated strict outer barriers, and the tensor threshold
shift K 7→ K − cg/2. Their generalized-support conclusion is Lemma 4.7; it will be used below
only on fixed compact sets with those same barrier conventions.
    Choose a smooth compactly supported offset C, with Cw ≤ C ≤ Cb , such that on the outward
leaf collars
             C = Cb − kB s    on black collars,        C = Cw + k B s   on white collars,
where Cb > 0 > Cw and kB > 0. Set
                            b− = cb − Cb < 0,      b+ = −cw − Cw > 0.                           (7.9)
The thresholds and these offsets will be made small in the order specified in Lemma 7.10.

7.4   Exact identities and the metric mass flux
We give the differential identities used in the deformation, including their dependence on the
spatially varying lapse profile. The argument in this subsection is local until the asymptotic
assumptions in Proposition 7.7 are imposed. In particular, none of the identities requires a
solution of the deformation equations to have been constructed in advance.

Asymptotic hypotheses for the flux calculation. For a tensor T , the notation T = Oj (r−γ )
means that its b-norm and the b-norms of its covariant derivatives through order j are O(r−γ ).
The following statement concerns each fixed choice of the profile parameters. Its constants need
not be uniform as those parameters vary.

Lemma 7.8 (Scalar-curvature decay from the end equation). Suppose the differentiated bounds
of Equation (7.35) hold, Rg + 6 = O(r−3−δ ), and

                                      2s > 3 + δ,        2β > 3 + δ.

Suppose also that the scalar end equation has the form
                                             3
                          ∆g Z + C(Z)|dZ|2g = (e4Z − 1) + O(r−3−δ ),                          (7.44)
                                             4
where C is smooth and bounded near zero for the fixed profiles. Then Equation (7.36) holds. In
the deformation equations one takes
                                                                            
                               C(Z) = p(Z) + 2 − d0 (Z) p(Z) + 1

on the tail with spatially constant n.

Proof. Since D = (1 − |w|2 )−1 , we have Z − t = O2 (r−2β ). The assumed end equation therefore
gives
                                               3
                           ∆g t + C(t)|dt|2g = (e4t − 1) + O(r−3−δ ).
                                               4
First apply the conformal scalar-curvature formula to e4t g:

                     e4t Re4t g + 6 = Rg + 6 − 8∆g t − 8|dt|2g + 6(e4t − 1)
                                  

                                      = Rg + 6 + 8 C(t) − 1 |dt|2g + O(r−3−δ ).
                                                                  


The gradient term is O(r−2s ) = O(r−3−δ ). The difference gb −e4t g = e4t D w♭ ⊗w♭ is O2 (r−2β ). The
local scalar-curvature formula, on the uniformly equivalent end metrics with bounded curvature,
then gives Rgb − Re4t g = O(r−2β ). This proves the required decay.

Remark 7.9. The scalar-curvature hypothesis in the mass proposition is essential to the convergence
argument. Bounds t = O2 (r−s ) for every s < 3, by themselves, would allow t = r−3 log r, whose
conformal time-flux integral diverges logarithmically. In the construction, Lemma 7.8 supplies
the missing integrability from the scalar equation. The estimates of the primitive residual are
then used separately to determine the sign of the limiting flux in Equation (7.39).

7.5   A specified continuation and uniform height control
Choose j ∈ C ∞ (R; [0, 1]) equal to zero on t ≤ 0 and one on t ≥ 1, and put

              D = Aχ {K(w, ·) + A(w)w},             Vϑ = u(4Aχ ∇Z + ϑD),         0 ≤ ϑ ≤ 1.



                                                    91
Before the final scalar stage the inner condition is
                             (Vϑ )ν
                                    = [1 − (1 − ϑ)j(t)][ B − (1 − ϑ)Dν ].                       (7.45)
                               u
The outer values are always f = Z = 0. The scalar source is always the expression Ξ in
Equation (7.16), evaluated using the current trace F .
    Here are explicit trace corrections. On a black collar take a negative multiple of a spatial
cutoff, a directional cutoff supported on w · νs < −1/2, and a nonincreasing height cutoff equal
to one near b− and zero near b+ . On a white collar use a positive multiple, a directional cutoff
supported on w · νs > 1/2, and a nondecreasing height cutoff supported near b+ . Their sum
is denoted by D0 (x, w, h). It is nondecreasing in h, vanishes in compatible directions, and is
nonpositive at sufficiently low heights and nonnegative at sufficiently high heights. Choose its
amplitudes larger than 2 sup∂Ω |H| + 1. The limiting directional inequalities at the assigned face
height then are
                        F (x, +ν, b) ≥ PB + H,      F (x, −ν, b) ≤ PB − H.                  (7.46)
These are evaluations at |w| = 1, hence d = χ = 0. Let b(x) be a compactly supported extension
of the face values, constant on smaller collars, and let D1 (x, w) = M1 w · νs on those collars, cut
off smoothly, where M1 > sup |H| + 1.
    The four stages are:
   (i) Decrease ϑ from one to zero, retaining F = h + C and the original face heights.
  (ii) Keep ϑ = 0 and the face heights, and set F = h + C + αD0 (x, w, h), with α increasing from
       zero to one.
 (iii) Keep ϑ = 0, prescribe h|∂Ω = (1 − ζ)b, and set

       F = h + (1 − ζ){C + D0 (x, w, h + ζb(x))} + ζ{trAχ K + D1 (x, w)},           0 ≤ ζ ≤ 1. (7.47)

 (iv) Keep the trace problem at ζ = 1, and multiply both Ξ and the right side of Equation (7.45)
      by a scalar decreasing from one to zero.
The prescriptions agree at their junctions. At fixed x, Z, df , each trace right side has derivative at
least one in h. The inequalities in Equation (7.46) persist in the third stage by convex combination.
In the last stage f = 0: at an extremum w = 0, D1 = 0, and the trace equation has the strict
height sign. Hence t = Z there.
Lemma 7.10 (Height, confinement, and the order of compact choices). For all sufficiently large
N , and all sufficiently distant outer truncations at fixed profiles, every smooth solution in the
first three stages satisfying t ≥ −ϵ obeys

                               |τ f | ≤ B,     |h| ≤ Br−β    (r ≥ r0 ).                         (7.48)

The outer normal slope obeys the differentiated rate of the same radial barrier, and t > −ϵ on
the outer face. The constants B, r0 may be chosen independently of collar widths and collar
derivatives, uniformly while the thresholds and correction amplitudes range in fixed bounded sets.
    The thresholds and offsets can consequently be fixed so that, on every physical solution obtained
below,
                                      |F tr K| + |∇C| ≤ 2ρ.                                     (7.49)
The fixed region on which n = N can be chosen to contain every compact sublevel set used in the
boundary slope argument.

                                                  92
Proof. At an interior extremum of f , w = 0, so A, D0 , and D1 contribute nothing to the trace
equation. In stage three it reads

                            tr(l Hess f ) = τ AR f + (1 − ζ)(C − tr K).

In the first two stages the same formula has ζ = 0. Since AR ≥ 1, the maximum principle and
the boundary values give

                           |τ f | ≤ max{|b− |, |b+ |, sup | tr K| + sup |C|}.

No derivative of a collar correction has been used. The necessary correction amplitudes are
uniformly bounded: on a black face H = cb − trT K, and the white face has the analogous bound.
All supports can be kept in a fixed compact enlargement by shortening the individual collars.

                                     a{−2 + χ(k − 2) + o(1)}

for the positive decreasing test. This is bounded above by a fixed negative multiple of a, uniformly

λ/AR and l0 have positive lower bounds at fixed profiles. Thus the negative Hessian contribution

                                                   93
Choose the two negative thresholds sufficiently close to zero, then their collars, and finally
sufficiently small offset amplitudes, so that

                    |(h + C) tr K| + |∇C| ≤ ρ on Q whenever b− ≤ h ≤ b+ .

The constants controlling derivatives of the now fixed collars need not be uniform in this choice.
The next lemma proves precisely the required physical height interval on Q. Outside Q the end
bound proves Equation (7.49), after making the support of C lie in Q. Finally enlarge the fixed
constant-n region so that Equation (7.48) rules out heights near b− and b+ beyond it. This radius
is fixed before N and R.

Proof. We verify the extension of Lemma 6.1 rather than assume an asymptotically flat hypothesis.
A putative failure supplies Ni → ∞ and outer radii tending to infinity. On the relevant compact

                                                l

                               tr(dϕ)⊥ Hess ϕ

at heights below k ′ , with b− < k ′ < 0 close to b− . The low-height D0 term is nonpositive. The

contact of the resulting low closed sublevel set. The end bound makes that set compact. After
adjoining the full black region, right continuity of the black threshold family excludes its meeting
any compact set away from that region. Reversing signs proves the upper separation, including
the empty-white-region case.
