7.6     Exclusion of the floor
Lemma 7.12 (Floor separation). A fixed sufficiently small δ0 > 0 and then a fixed finite integer
P0 can be chosen so that, for all sufficiently large N and all sufficiently distant truncations at
fixed profiles, no smooth solution anywhere in the continuation can satisfy t ≥ −ϵ and attain
t = −ϵ. Neither choice requires a fixed-profile Schauder constant.
Proof. At an interior contact dt = 0, Hess t ≥ 0. The Gauss equation of the graph in the
Riemannian warped product g + l2 ds20 gives
        1 4t     1                   tr⊥ Hessx l
                                                                                          
          e Rĝ ≤ Rg − v Ric(e, e) +                          + S S − K + d−1 A(w)w ⊗ w ,               (7.51)
        2        2                        l
where
                               1           1 tf 2
                     S(B) = (tr⊥ B)2 − |B⊥⊥       | + dBee tr⊥ B − d|Be⊥ |2 .
                               4           2
To obtain this formula, the spatial lapse derivatives contribute w ⊗ A + A ⊗ w to the graph
second form, and its vertical connection contributes d−1 A(w)w ⊗ w. The remaining p Hess t lapse
curvature and conformal Hessian terms are nonpositive at a contact. The rank-one term changes
S by only vA(w) tr⊥ (S − K).
    We give the derivative ledger that controls the additional terms. At a floor contact, p = n,
dx p = dn, and

        A = s − ϵn d log n,         ∇w = H f Ad + dA ⊗ w,            d log u = H f (w, ·) + (1 + v)A.

                                                     95
Positive-order derivatives of log n are bounded. Thus Hessx l/l, A, and the needed spatial
derivatives have fixed-degree polynomial bounds in n, uniformly in position. The factor ϵ < 1
introduces no larger growth. Moreover
                             4+p        4d
                       ∂p          =           ,        dv = 2d{H f (w, ·) + vA}
                            4 + pv   (4 + pv)2
at contact. Therefore the derivatives of Aχ always carry a d factor in the uncontrolled axial
Hessian direction. In d log u, that direction contracts with χ in the flux. The same is true for the
new drift Aχ (A(w)w) = χA(w)w. A spatial derivative of A in this drift is contracted axially and
retains χ.
    The algebraic floor comparison of Lemma 4.13, applied to S, and Equation (7.51) therefore
give, with universal c > 0, small prescribed η > 0, and fixed finite powers m, the bounds
                                                  
               T − S S − K + d−1 A(w)w ⊗ w ≥ cT − Cnm (ρ0 + v),
                                              w(F ) ≥ τ AR aσ − ηT − Cη nm (ρ0 + v),
                                      u−1 | div(uD)| ≤ ηT + Cη nm (ρ0 + v).
Here |K|2 + |∇K|2 ≲ ρ0 , and the height estimate bounds the squared end height by the same
weight. For example a|∇K| is bounded by v + |∇K|2 . The derivative of h = τ AR f gives the
displayed positive term and h w(d log AR ), whose absolute value is at most Ca|h| ≤ C(v + ρ0 ) for
this floor estimate. Derivatives of D0 , D1 , b, C are fixed compact coefficients; the derivative in h
is nonnegative and at least one. These observations enumerate all new spatial terms beyond the
flat floor calculation, including dx p, which is not zero here.
    Combining these bounds with Equation (7.18) and the constraint gives
                             u−1 div Vϑ ≥ 2δ0 T + τ AR aσ − Cnm (ρ0 + v)                        (7.52)
for fixed sufficiently small δ0 . The cosmological constants cancel in deriving this lower bound:
8πµ − Rg /2 − 3 = Qg (K)/2. In the prescribed source the remaining explicit term 3(e4t − 1) is
negative at the floor, where d0 = δ0 and m0 = 1. Choose P0 > m + 1 and N sufficiently large that
its penalty dominates the error and 2ρ + ρ0 . Equation (7.52) then contradicts the scalar equation.
    Outer contacts were excluded in Lemma 7.10. At an inner contact j = 0, so Equation (7.45)
restores the full flux condition. Since A = 0 on the collar, its face identity is
                                        d(H + 4∂ν t) = −NB d.
It forces ∂ν t < 0, contrary to a minimum. In the final scalar stage f = 0, t = Z. At a floor
minimum the unscaled source is negative after the same penalty is enlarged; at positive scale the
inner derivative is negative as well. At zero scale the homogeneous mixed problem gives Z = 0 by
multiplication by Z. This proves separation for all stages.

7.7    Bounds, a compact equation, and exhaustion
Proposition 7.13 (Existence at fixed profiles). Choose δ0 , P0 as in Lemma 7.12. There is a
fixed sufficiently large B0 , followed by a fixed exponent a satisfying Equation (7.13), such that for
every sufficiently large N the physical system has a smooth solution on the infinite exterior with
t ≥ −ϵ. It is a smooth compact limit of solutions on sufficiently distant spherical truncations. At
fixed profiles, Z, t, D, and all their derivatives on hyperbolic unit patches are bounded; derivatives
of f are bounded after multiplication by the local value of λ. These bounds are uniform in outer
truncation. Moreover Rgb ≥ −6, and its inner boundary has strictly negative mean curvature.

                                                   96
Proof. We first prove estimates for all smooth floor-admissible solutions of the continuation,
without assuming their existence.

The principal divergence at high levels. Put B = A + p dt. Differentiation gives
           ∇w = H f Ad + dB ⊗ w,          d log u = (p + 2 + pv)dt + H f (w, ·) + (1 + v)A.
At fixed profiles all explicit coefficients and their needed derivatives are bounded on t ≥ −ϵ. The
derivatives in the axial Hessian direction carry d or χ, as in the proof of Lemma 7.12; the same
holds for pt dt, because ∂p ((4 + p)/(4 + pv)) = 4d/(4 + pv)2 . The derivative of A in the drift
χA(w)w is contracted axially and retains χ. Coercivity of Equation (7.15) and Young’s Inequality
consequently give
                      u−1 | div(uD)| ≤ ηT + CN ,        4|⟨A, dt⟩Ad | ≤ ηT + CN
for any fixed η > 0. These statements bound contracted expressions; they do not assert a bound
for the unweighted axial entry of H f .
    On a sufficiently high Z level, either t is large or D is large. In the first case 3(e4t − 1) absorbs
the bounded errors. In the second case
                                                      τ AR v
                                          τ AR aσ =        √
                                                       λl0 d
does so. At fixed profiles τ AR /λ has a positive lower bound on the whole exterior. The floor
penalty is bounded by a fixed-profile constant and is absorbed in the same manner. Choosing η
below a fixed fraction of δ0 yields

At a face the remaining trace terms have only T and k|∇Γ Z| growth. Young’s Inequality
absorbs them in the left side of Equation (7.55), at cost CN k on the right. This step requires no
new small factor τ /ℓ.
    The Sobolev Inequality iterates 2k to 6k on nested patches. Interpolation with the bounded
L2 norm closes the iteration: the larger-patch supremum has exponent 1 − 1/k∗ < 1, so geometric
radius gaps give a uniform smaller-patch supremum. This includes inner faces. Equation (7.53)
excludes a higher interior maximum elsewhere, and the outer value is zero. Hence Z is uniformly
bounded above in stage one.
    In stages two and three there is no drift. The high-level source excludes an interior maximum.
At an inner face the upper f -slope bound forces t > 1 if Z is sufficiently large. Then j = 1
and the conormal derivative is zero, contradicting the boundary point principle. In stage four
f = 0, and the same argument works at positive scale; at zero scale Z = 0 by its energy identity.
Consequently
                                 −ϵ ≤ t ≤ Z ≤ CN ,        D ≤ CN
throughout the continuation. On compact sets |df | ≤ CN ; on the end the same bound holds for
λ|df |.

Classical estimates. On a hyperbolic unit patch centered at x0 , put c = λ(x0 ) and frescaled = cf .
Also divide the entire scalar divergence equation, its total flux, and its prescribed conormal datum
by the same constant c. In local coordinates its leading matrix is then
                                             p
                                       a
                                       e = 4 det g (u/c)Aχ .

The ratio λ/c is uniformly bounded above and below on the patch, with bounded logarithmic
derivatives. At fixed capped profiles, u/c and χ have positive upper and lower bounds on the
range just proved. The divided drift and source have the same normalization, and division by a
constant creates no derivative term. At fixed profiles the trace coefficients are uniformly elliptic
and bounded on the range just proved, and its normalized source is bounded. The rank-one
estimate of Theorem 5.1 gives a positive Hölder exponent for the rescaled gradient and
                            ˆ
                                |D2 frescaled |2 ≤ CN r1+γN (γN > 0).
                              Br

After t = t(x, Z, df ) is substituted in the scalar equation, its source is bounded by CN (1 + |DZ|2 +
|D2 frescaled |2 ). The drift flux and prescribed inner conormal value are bounded. Lemma 5.6
therefore gives Hölder continuity of Z, including the disjoint Dirichlet and conormal faces. The
coupled bootstrap of Proposition 5.7 applies: the trace coefficients and source are now Hölder;
after solving the trace equation for two derivatives of f , the scalar equation has Hölder leading
coefficients and natural quadratic growth in DZ. On a face its normal derivative is a smooth
function of x, Z, f, df , because df is normal. There is no second derivative of f or derivative of Z
in that boundary function. These checks give every local classical bound, uniformly in truncation.

A trace solution for arbitrary inputs. The preceding estimates apply only to prospective
fixed points. To define the compact map, fix a finite truncation and a bounded input Z ∈ C 1,b ,
without a floor assumption. At large σ = |df |, the defining relation for t gives
                                                                       √
                       4Z − log(λσ)                     −8/(n+4)         D
                   t=               + O(1),       χ≍σ            ,          ≍ σ.
                           n+4                                          l
Since n ≥ N ≥ 4, χ ≥ c/(1 + σ). The new coefficient A has growth O(log(2 + σ)), but its
normalized trace contribution has size

                                       Cσχ|A| ≤ CN (1 + σ).

All other terms have the same linear growth on the bounded height range. The principal matrix
is independent of the height, the normalized source has strict positive height derivative, and all
expressions are smooth and direction free on bounded ranges. These are exactly the hypotheses of
Proposition 6.8. It gives the unique C 3,b trace solution, continuous in the input and continuation
parameter. Its constants may depend on this finite truncation; this causes no difficulty in defining
the map.
    Freeze its coefficients, drift, and source, and solve the linear mixed problem for a new Z,
leaving only its principal gradient unfrozen. The outer Dirichlet face gives coercivity. The output
is bounded in C 2,b on bounded input sets, hence the map is compact in C 1,b . The set defined by
min t(x, Z, df ) > −ϵ and a sufficiently large norm cutoff is open in the parameter–input product.
Lemma 7.12 and the preceding bounds exclude boundary fixed points. The endpoint map is
zero, and zero is admissible. Lemma 6.10 therefore gives a physical fixed point. First take N

sufficiently large for the finite list of polynomial absorptions and compact separations; then take
each outer radius sufficiently large for all fixed-profile barriers. The a priori bounds are uniform
in that outer radius, so a diagonal compact limit solves the physical system with t ≥ −ϵ.

7.8   Decay and the four limiting fluxes
Lemma 7.14 (Differentiated end estimates). For each fixed N , the solution of Proposition 7.13
satisfies
                 λf = Oj (r−β ),    w = Oj (r−β ),     Z − t = Oj (r−2β )
for every j, and Z, t = Oj (r−γ ) for every γ < 3. Furthermore

                                          Rgb + 6 = Oj (r−3−δ ).

All four metric fluxes of gb exist. They satisfy Equation (7.37), and the time component satisfies
Equation (7.39).

Proof. The profiles are fixed throughout this proof. On the ultimate tail n = n∞ is constant and
A = s, and AR is proportional to r. The height bound of Lemma 7.10 therefore gives λf = O(r−β ).
On each hyperbolic unit patch rescale f by the central value of λ. The trace equation is uniformly
elliptic there by Proposition 7.13. Its inhomogeneous term at f = 0 is Oj (r−κ ); the remaining
zeroth-order term is a bounded coefficient times the rescaled f . The local linear estimates for this
fixed smooth solution consequently give λf = Oj (r−β ), using β < κ. The equations for w and D
give the next two estimates. The same argument holds on the outer truncations with uniform
boundary estimates at their zero Dirichlet face.
    Here is the scalar expansion before assuming decay of Z. Since t is bounded and n∞ is fixed,
the substitution t = Z + Oj (r−2β ) has uniformly bounded differentiated coefficients. In the flux,

                   Aχ = I + Oj (r−2β ),        u−1 du = s + (p + 2)dZ + Oj (r−2β ).

The tensor part of S has order Oj (r−β ). In Equation (7.15), the part quadratic in dZ is 4(p+1)|dZ|2
to error Oj (r−2β ). Every mixed term between S and dt has an additional factor a: its two possible
forms are a⟨M, y⟩ and aq1 xt . Thus these terms, including their differentiated bounds, have
order r−2β . The tensor drift K(w, ·) has order r−κ−β . The leading lapse term 4⟨s, dZ⟩ from
u−1 div(4u∇Z) cancels the explicit lapse term on the right side of the scalar equation.

    The capillary term is Oj (r−2β ). Even before its support disappears, the penalty contributes
Oj (r−3−δ ) + Oj (r−2β ), because n∞ is fixed and all derivatives of t are bounded. Since 2β > 4 + δ,
the resulting equation is

7.10    The full-cut inequality and the invariant mass
Proof of Theorem 7.1. Fix one prepared data set and the compact black–white exterior used in
the construction. Every full enclosing cut in this smaller exterior is a full enclosing cut in the
prepared original exterior. Since t ≥ −ϵ and the graph term is nonnegative, gb ≥ e−4ϵ g. On
each tangent two-plane its area density is therefore at least e−4ϵ times the g-density. Taking the
infimum over all full cuts gives
                                            b gb) ≥ e−4ϵ Amin (S; g).
                                    Amin (∂ Ω;

This comparison uses the entire intrinsic boundary of each cut, including the original boundary
when the exterior itself is chosen.
    By Proposition 7.13 and Lemma 7.14, (Ω, b gb, 0) has one complete AH end with the required
tensor-norm decay and weighted scalar-curvature integrability, all four finite metric fluxes, scalar
curvature at least −6, and strictly mean-negative boundary. Thus the time-symmetric case
of Theorem 3.1 applies in the designated chart, irrespective of the causal character of its flux
covector. Together with Proposition 7.15, it gives
                                             s                                              !
                                                 e−4ϵ Amin (S; g)    e−4ϵ Amin (S; g)
               p0 (g) + oN (1) ≥ p0 (gb) ≥                        1+                  .
                                                       16π                 4π

Send N → ∞; then ϵ = B0 log N/N → 0. Finally remove the strict preparation using Lemma 7.2,
which preserves the four metric flux limits and the full-cut infimum. This proves Equation (7.1).




                                                     105
Theorem 7.16 (The AH mass inequality). If the metric flux covector of the data in Theorem 7.1
is future timelike, then
                                                        s
                                                            Amin (S; g)    Amin (S; g)
                          q                                                             
                 mAH :=       p20 − p21 − p22 − p23 ≥                   1+             .
                                                              16π             4π
Proof. The four static potentials form the defining Lorentz representation of the orientation
and time-orientation preserving hyperbolic isometry group. The metric flux is linear in the
potential. Consequently, composing the designated end chart with such an isometry acts on its
four components by the corresponding Lorentz transformation. This follows directly by changing
variables in the flux formula: the background metric and its connection are preserved, and
the replacement sphere exhaustion has the same limit because the weighted scalar-linearization
divergence is absolutely integrable. The quadratic remainder is integrable under the stated decay
exponent > 3/2. The tensor decay and weighted integrability conditions persist, since two radial
functions related by a fixed hyperbolic isometry are comparable far out.
    A future timelike covector admits a proper future Lorentz transformation taking it to
(mAH , 0, 0, 0). Apply Theorem 7.1 in this chart. The geometry of the exterior, its future boundary
signs, and its intrinsic full-cut area infimum have not changed. This proves the assertion. Only a
hyperbolic change of spatial end coordinates was used.

    This completes the proof of Theorem 1.1.


8     First variations of equality and the original-data adjoint
In this section S is connected, is an outermost future MOTS, and is outer area-minimizing in the
full-cut sense. We assume equality in Theorem 7.16. All variations below are variations of the
original pair (g, K) on the original manifold Ω. Nearby data need satisfy only the hypotheses of
the numerical inequality; their boundaries need not remain outermost or area-minimizing. Here τ
again denotes the original asymptotically hyperbolic decay exponent in Definition 2.1.
    Choose a balanced spatial chart, so that p0 = mAH and pi = 0 at the data under consideration.

Theorem 8.1 (Causal Killing data produced by equality). There are a smooth function u and a

    We prove the theorem without assuming a stationary development. The Lorentzian metric in
its statement will be constructed only after the multiplier fields have been obtained and shown to
be smooth and positive.

8.1   The envelope derivative for full enclosing area
Extend g smoothly across a short collar of S, and fix the entire inner side of that collar as an
obstacle. This auxiliary extension is used only for perimeter minimization. It is not required to
satisfy constraints. Minimize among bounded filled sets containing the obstacle, taking perimeter
in the extended open collar and the original exterior. Thus the obstacle itself has boundary S
and perimeter Areag (S): the convention does not erase S when the exterior competitor is D = Ω.
    Bounded complementary pockets can be filled. Their removal from the exterior component
cannot increase perimeter, and any nonempty pocket of a regular minimizer would contribute
removable positive perimeter. The remaining complement is the connected exterior containing
the end. Conversely, every full cut in the original definition gives a filled competitor in this
formulation. A compact C 1,1 obstacle boundary can be pushed slightly into the exterior by a
collar vector field and then smoothed in graph charts. These operations preserve separation and
make its areas converge. The perimeter infimum therefore equals the infimum over the smooth
full cuts of Definition 2.2.
    Let M denote the family of boundaries attaining this perimeter infimum for g.
Lemma 8.2 (Compact minimizing family and area derivative). Let gs be a smooth metric path
through g, with its first two parameter derivatives bounded relative to g, and with a common
                               ′
asymptotically hyperbolic C 2,α bound of positive decay. Then, after restricting the parameter
interval, the minimizing boundaries for gs lie in a common compact set. The family M is compact
for indicator L1 convergence and tangent-plane area-measure convergence. If H = ġ0 , then
                                                                 ˆ
                      d                  ′             ′       1
                           A(s) = min aT (H),         aT (H) =     trT H dAg .            (8.10)
                     ds 0+        T ∈M                         2 T

                                               107
Off the obstacle, minimizing boundaries that meet have the same tangent plane. This common
plane is continuous on their union locally away from S.
Proof. Large coordinate spheres have mean curvature 2 + o(1), uniformly for these paths. Fix
a radius beyond which this mean curvature is positive and the obstacle is absent. On a larger
compact truncation, the direct method for perimeter gives an obstacle minimizer. Its boundary
cannot have a maximal radius in the strictly mean-convex region: at a free maximum it is minimal
and lies on the inner side of a strictly mean-convex coordinate sphere, in contradiction with the
local comparison principle. Contact with the outer truncation can likewise be moved inward to
lower area. Thus every such minimizer lies inside the same fixed radius. Truncations containing
any prescribed smooth competitor show that their minimum is the full infimum.
    The local obstacle regularity theorem [15, Regularity Theorem 1.3(iii)] applies here to pure
perimeter, a smooth obstacle, a smooth metric and ambient dimension three. It gives C 1,1
boundaries and smooth minimal free parts. Part (iii) is used for this individual regularity. For a
fixed 0 < α0 ≤ 1/2, the local C 1,α0 estimates in Part (ii) are uniform: the obstacle bounds and
positive metric C 1 bounds are uniform for the family gs .
    Only away from S do we need density estimates. Here is a local proof with uniform constants.
In a sufficiently small ball centered at a boundary point, compare a minimizing set E with
E \ Bρ and E ∪ Bρ , filling any unnecessary pockets afterwards. The ball misses the obstacle. For
f (ρ) = Vol(E ∩ Bρ ), minimality and coarea give, for almost every ρ,

                          P (E; Bρ ) ≤ f ′ (ρ),     P (E ∩ Bρ ) ≤ 2f ′ (ρ).

Uniform local isoperimetry implies f ′ (ρ) ≥ cf (ρ)2/3 , hence f (ρ) ≥ cρ3 . The same argument
applies to the complement. Relative isoperimetry then gives P (E; Bρ ) ≥ cρ2 ; the comparison
above and the uniform sphere-area bound give P (E; Bρ ) ≤ Cρ2 . These constants are uniform
on compact sets disjoint from S. Perimeter compactness now gives subsequential indicator
convergence. Lower semicontinuity, together with testing a fixed minimizer, gives convergence of
perimeters, including for varying metrics.
    Here is the measure-continuity detail needed for differentiation. In a fixed orthonormal frame
let DχEj be the vector normal measures. Their weak convergence and convergence of total
variations imply weak convergence of their variation measures: any weak limit dominates |DχE |,
and equality of total masses eliminates the excess. Approximate the limiting unit normal in the
square-integral norm of this measure by continuous vector fields. For such a field v, expand
                  ˆ                       ˆ                        ˆ
                             2                     2
                     |νj − v| d|DχEj | = (1 + |v| ) d|DχEj | − 2 v · dDχEj .

Both terms converge. Continuous approximation and uniform continuity then give convergence for
every continuous function of position and unit normal, and hence for functions of the unoriented
tangent plane. A partition of unity proves the assertion globally. Uniform metric convergence
converts varying-metric perimeters to this fixed-metric argument.
   On every rectifiable cut of bounded area, the area-element expansion is uniform:

                      Areags (T ) = Areag (T ) + s a′T (H) + O(s2 ) Areag (T ).            (8.11)

A fixed minimizing cut gives the upper one-sided bound in (8.10). For the lower bound take
minimizers Ts for gs , pass along any sequence s ↓ 0 to a member T ∈ M, and use
                           A(s) − A(0)
                                       ≥ a′Ts (H) + O(s) −→ a′T (H).
                                s

                                                  108
Compactness and continuity of a′T (H) prove the formula.
   Finally, the union and intersection of two filled minimizing sets remain obstacle competitors.
Perimeter submodularity gives

                        P (E1 ∪ E2 ) + P (E1 ∩ E2 ) ≤ P (E1 ) + P (E2 ) = 2A.

Both terms on the left are at least A, so both are minimizers. Different tangent planes at an
intersection would give a corner in one of them, contradicting interior regularity. To check
continuity on the union, take points on a sequence of minimizers converging away from the
obstacle. The two phase-volume density bounds prevent disappearance of the limiting point
under indicator convergence, so it lies on a limiting minimizer. The uniform local C 1,α0 estimates
and strict perimeter convergence then give tangent-plane convergence there. The common-plane
property makes this independent of the chosen sequence.

Lemma 8.3 (Strict conformal direction). Choose 3/2 < β < min{2, τ } and 0 < δ < min{1, 2β −
3}. There are smooth positive weights w, w2 , D, with w = r−3−δ , w2 = r−β on a far end and

                                   q
                  (−∆ + 3)ϕ = D w22 + |dϕ|2 + w,                            ∂ν ϕ = −1.            (8.13)

It has finite fluxes against all four static potentials. In particular,
                                                           (−∆ + 3)ϕ
                         (−∆ + 3)ϕ ≥ |K||dϕ| + w,                    −→ 1.

For Q = (4ϕg, 2ϕK), on the active set one has
                  ′                                    ′               ′
                 CQ = 8 (3 − ∆)ϕ + K(v, ∇ϕ) ,          CQ /w −→ 8,     θQ = −4 on S.              (8.14)

Proof. Solve (8.13) on spherical truncations with outer Dirichlet value zero. Continue both the
source and the inner Neumann value from zero. The gradient term can be written b · dϕ + f ,
where
                                 |b| ≤ D,    0 ≤ f ≤ Dw2 + w.
The operator is consequently −∆ − b · ∇ + 3. A fixed collar function supplies the prescribed
outward Neumann derivative, which is +1; adding a sufficiently large constant dominates its
bounded compactly supported derivatives and the source. The maximum principle gives a bound
independent of the truncation and continuation parameter. Beyond a fixed sphere,

                          (3 − ∆b )r−β = (3 − β)(1 + β)r−β + O(r−β−2 ).

                                                 109
The decaying drift and metric errors preserve its positive leading coefficient. Comparison with a
multiple of this function, using the already bounded value on the fixed sphere and zero outer
data, gives |ϕ| ≤ Cr−β .
    Lemma A.1, followed by Sobolev embedding and the one-sided Schauder estimate, gives the
same weighted bound through two derivatives. At the fixed inner face use the corresponding
Neumann estimates. The linearized equation has bounded drift, zeroth-order coefficient 3,
homogeneous Neumann data at the inner face and homogeneous Dirichlet data at the outer face.
Its kernel is zero by the maximum principle, so the usual elliptic Fredholm alternative makes it
invertible. The a priori estimates close the method of continuity on each truncation. Exhaustion
and local compactness then produce the asserted decaying solution, smooth on compact sets
including the one-sided boundary.
    The additional source is O(r−2β ) = o(w), proving the ratio claim. Replacing ∆g by ∆b costs
O(r−τ −β ). Both this error and r−2β have finite integrals with weight V0 dVb , as does w. The
scalar linearization identity
                        divb U(V, 4ϕb) = 8V (3 − ∆b )ϕ,     Hessb V = V b,
therefore proves existence of all the conformal fluxes. Replacing b by g in 4ϕb costs order r−τ −β ,
beyond the mass order.
   For the exact conformal transformation gs = e4sϕ g, Ks = e2sϕ K, direct differentiation gives
                              ′

The first term vanishes on Z. The expansion transforms as θs = e−2sϕ (θ + 4s∂ν ϕ); since θ = 0 on
S, its derivative is −4.

    In addition to Q, take all compact variations, including arbitrary one-sided jets at S, and the
following end variations, cut off near the compact interior:
  1. (4ψg, 2ψK), where ψ = r−3 f (ω) and f ∈ C ∞ (S2 );
  2. pure tensor variations (0, P ) whose trace reversal with respect to b has only mixed block
                                    (P − (trb P )b)νb A = r−3 WA (ω)
     in radial and spherical orthonormal scaling, for arbitrary smooth one-forms W on S2 .
Their constraint derivatives are o(w) on the active rays. Indeed the conformal radial indicial
term cancels, leaving an O(r−5 ) background scalar term and products with the decaying original
coefficients. For the mixed block, its radial derivative is −3(V0 /r)r−3 WA ; the sphere connection
terms give +3(V0 /r)r−3 WA . Thus its tangential background divergence is zero, while its normal
divergence is r−4 divσ W . Metric and tensor errors cost O(r−3−τ ). The choice δ < 1 proves the
assertion.
    Let V be the real linear space generated by these tests. The coefficient a(H, P ) of Q is well
defined even if Z is compact. Indeed, on the full unit ball bundle, C = O(r−τ ), uniformly in v,
and the conformal identity in the preceding proof gives
                                   ′

                                     = 8 + o(1) −      = 8 + o(1).
                                  w                 w
Here τ + β > 2β > 3 + δ, so the last term tends to zero. The other generators have normalized
full-ball derivative tending to zero by the same end calculations (and compact variations vanish
there). Applying these limits to any linear relation among the generators forces the coefficient of
Q to vanish. The active-ray statement is its restriction when active rays exist.

   Suppose its image contained a function positive everywhere. Write its direction as aQ+(H0 , P0 ).

For clarity, the non-strict end metric and tensor tests are O(r−3 ), their background constraint
terms are O(r−4 ) + O(r−3−τ ), and their products with the strict direction are O(r−3−β ). The
strict direction’s own quadratic errors are O(r−2β ). All are controlled by w. Applying the exact
conformal law after the intermediate variation gives these bounds without differentiating the
nonnegative original constraint into an uncontrolled error. One may compose the two isometries
of unit balls in this calculation: at an active unit ray a difference of isometric identifications is
tangent to the unit sphere and is annihilated by J; at a vacuum ray J = 0.
     On the remaining compact ball bundle, positivity of the derivative on the closed active set
persists on a neighborhood of it. The original constraint has a positive minimum on the compact
complement. Taylor’s formula thus gives the DEC for sufficiently small s > 0 everywhere. The
boundary has strictly negative future expansion. The same estimates are integrable with weight
V0 , and the flux divergence identity proves differentiability of all four fluxes. The covector remains
future timelike by continuity. Thus (8.2) applies to this path.

                                     c min a′T (H) − 16πp′0 > 0.
                                      T ∈M

By Lemma 8.2 and (8.1), the right derivative at zero of the difference in (8.2) is negative. That
difference is initially zero and is nonnegative for s > 0, a contradiction.

   The image of (8.16) is therefore disjoint from the open positive cone of continuous functions.
Hahn–Banach separation gives a nonzero continuous functional, nonnegative on nonnegative
functions and annihilating this linear image. The Riesz Representation Theorem gives finite
nonnegative measures on its three compact pieces. The objective piece has positive mass: otherwise
evaluation on Q, strictly positive on both other pieces, would contradict annihilation. Normalize
that mass to one. Divide the finite measure on finite active rays by w and call it L; absorb eight
times the end-point atom into z. Rearranging the identity gives (8.15).

8.4    Removing the interior area multipliers
Lemma 8.5 (Normal slice tests). For every s ∈ Cc∞ (int Ω), there are compact variations (Hϵ , Pϵ )
with Hϵ = 2sK, supported in one fixed compact set, whose active-ray constraint derivatives tend
uniformly to zero.

Proof. On a compact neighborhood of the support, choose a Gaussian Lorentzian collar

                         gϵ = −dt2 + gϵ (t),           gϵ (0) = g,    ∂t gϵ (0) = 2K.

Prescribe its second time jets so that
                        1                                            J ⊗J
                          (Ggϵ − 3gϵ )ij = Dϵ,ij ,           Dϵ =             at t = 0.
                       8π                                            µ+ϵ
The spatial Einstein tensor depends on the freely chosen normal derivative of K by a nonzero
multiple of trace reversal. In dimension three trace reversal has eigenvalues 1 and −2; it is
invertible. The remaining terms are fixed spatial jets. Thus these smooth second jets can be
prescribed, and a quadratic time extension with a cutoff realizes a smooth Lorentzian metric on a
sufficiently short collar. The normal and mixed components of the displayed tensor are µ, J, by
the constraints.
    Set D = J ⊗ J/µ where µ > 0, and set it to zero at vacuum. The DEC gives

                                 |Dϵ | ≤ µ,         |∇Dϵ | ≤ 2|∇J| + |∇µ|.

At a zero of the smooth nonnegative function µ, its differential vanishes; |Ji | ≤ µ then gives
dJi = 0 there also. The displayed derivative bound proves that D is C 1 across vacuum with zero
derivative there. Splitting any compact set into {µ ≥ δ} and {µ < δ} proves Dϵ → D in C 1 .
    Vary the slice by graphs t = as(x), differentiating at a = 0. The metric derivative is 2sK. At
a nonvacuum active ray, µ = |J|, v = −J ♯ /µ, and ζ = n + v is null. The limiting prescribed stress
has the form
                         µ−1 γ ⊗ γ,     γ(n) = µ, γ|T Ω = J, γ(ζ) = 0.



                                                       112
The covector γ is nonnegative on future causal vectors. Parallel transport ζ in any spatial
direction using the spacetime connection. Its contraction with γ is nonnegative and has a zero, so
its derivative there is zero. Consequently the spatial covariant derivatives of the limiting stress,
contracted in one slot with ζ, vanish.
    Let Tϵ = (Ggϵ − 3gϵ )/(8π). Contracted Bianchi, before taking the limit, gives
                                                     X
                                  (∇n Tϵ )(n, ζ) =       (∇ei Tϵ )(ei , ζ).
                                                     i

The right side tends to zero by the compact C 1 convergence just proved. Thus the required
normal derivative of the constraint combination tends to zero too. Differentiating its arguments
causes no additional limiting term: the first argument is paired against a tensor with ζ in its
kernel, and the second argument remains null; at activity γ is proportional to ζ ♭ , so nullity kills its
differentiated contraction. At vacuum the tensor and its spatial derivatives vanish, and Bianchi
gives the same conclusion for every vector in the closed unit ball. All expressions use only the
fixed compact jets and Dϵ , ∇Dϵ ; the convergence is uniform, including near vacuum.

Lemma 8.6 (Concentration on the original horizon). The measure ω in Lemma 8.4 is concentrated
on the single cut S.

Proof. Apply Lemma 8.5 in (8.15). Mass, expansion and end-point terms are zero. The constraint
measure is finite over the fixed compact support, so the limit gives
                        ˆ ˆ
                               s trT K dAg dω(T ) = 0    (s ∈ Cc∞ (int Ω)).
                          M   T
                                                        ´
Off S, form the positive aggregate area measure b = M dAT dω(T ). Lemma 8.2 makes F (x) =
trΠ(x) K(x), for the common tangent plane Π(x), a single continuous function on the union of the
cuts locally away from S. The last identity is F b = 0. Fubini on a countable compact exhaustion
shows that, for ω-almost every cut, trT K = 0 almost everywhere off S. Such a cut is smooth
minimal there, so continuity gives HT = trT K = 0 throughout its free part.
    At contact, write the cut and S as C 1,1 graphs with the same orientation. Their second
derivatives agree almost everywhere on the coincidence set: apply twice the fact that the derivative
of a Lipschitz function vanishing on a measurable set is zero almost everywhere on that set. The
cut therefore solves HT + trT K = 0 almost everywhere across contact as well as away from it.
This is a uniformly elliptic quasilinear graph equation. Its coefficients are Hölder after the C 1,1
bound; interior elliptic regularity and differentiation make the graph smooth.
    If this smooth MOTS touches S, their one-sided difference satisfies a linear elliptic equation
with bounded coefficients. The strong comparison principle gives local coincidence. The contact
set is thus open and closed in connected S, so the cut contains S. Its full area is already A,
excluding additional positive-area components. If it does not touch S, it is a smooth compact
full enclosing MOTS entirely in the interior, after filling removable pockets. This contradicts
outermostness. Hence almost every cut is exactly S.

The following version records the cosmological constant explicitly, for later use on annuli. Set

   To exhibit finite type, write Bij = ∇(i Xj) = −uKij . Commuting covariant derivatives gives

At a zero of u ≥ 0, du = 0; domination |X| ≤ u also gives X = dX = 0. Nonzero moments
consequently have positive lapse everywhere.

Lemma 8.8 (Compactness of normalized adjoint jets). Suppose smooth coefficients in Lemma 8.7
converge on compact subsets of a connected open manifold, and nonzero causal adjoints are defined
on an exhaustion containing a fixed point p. Normalize their full first jets at p to have norm one.
A subsequence converges smoothly on compact sets to a nonzero causal adjoint for the limiting
coefficients. The same conclusion holds for a parameter family on a fixed open manifold.

Proof. The jet at p cannot be zero by continuation. On any fixed compact set, a finite family
of coordinate neighborhoods and paths from p has bounded lengths and uniformly bounded
coefficients in (8.24). Grönwall’s Inequality gives uniform first-jet bounds there. The equations and
local elliptic estimates give bounds of every order on smaller compact sets. Diagonal compactness
gives the limit. Its jet at p still has norm one, and causality is a closed pointwise condition.
Lemma 8.7 supplies positive limiting lapse.

8.6   Boundary atoms and hyperbolic normalization
By Lemma 8.6, the area term in (8.15) is c a′S (H). Thus compact interior tests have zero pairing,
and Lemma 8.7, with Λc = −3, applies. It gives (8.4)–(8.6) in the interior.

Lemma 8.9 (One-sided continuation and normalization). The moment fields extend smoothly
to S, their original measure moments have no part supported on S, and they satisfy (8.3). In
particular they are nonzero and their lapse is positive in the interior.

Proof. In a smooth normal collar of S, the coefficient in (8.24) is smooth up to the face. Solve its
linear ODE down each collar segment from a fixed interior surface. Smooth dependence on its
footpoint gives a smooth extension; ODE uniqueness identifies it with the original field in the
collar.


                                                115
    Subtract integration by parts of these smooth interior fields from the compact multiplier
identity. Take a metric variation whose value and full first jet vanish on S, and set the tensor
variation to zero. All area, expansion and integrated boundary terms vanish. The remaining
boundary scalar moment tests −∂ν2 trS H, which is arbitrary, by choosing a prescribed s2 tangential
trace in collar coordinates. Momentum and frame terms involve only lower jets and are zero.
Thus the scalar moment has no boundary part. Its domination of the vector moment eliminates
that part as well.
    We next derive end behavior without imposing growth on the measures. Express the first-jet
system in a b-parallel radial frame, with η = arsinh r. At (b, 0) its lapse system is Hessb u = ub
and its shift system is hyperbolic Killing transport. Their radial propagators have norm at most

ambient linear functions and Lorentz generators on the hyperboloid. The actual coefficient differs
by O(e−τ η ); only two metric derivatives and one tensor derivative occur. Variation of constants
gives                                             ˆ η

8.7   Stationary curvature and free boundary variations
Completion of the proof of Theorem 8.1. Since u > 0, (8.7) is a smooth Lorentzian metric on the
open exterior product. Its coefficients are independent of t, so ξ = ∂t is Killing. Its future normal
is n = u−1 (∂t − X); the future-normal convention fixed after Equation (2.5) gives

                                      1           1
                                      2 Ln g = − 2u LX g = K

by (8.4). Thus both induced tensors are the original ones.
    We verify its curvature without a spacetime existence assumption. The Gauss–Codazzi and
lapse identities, obtained by differentiating the displayed metric, give for spatial slots

                Ricg,ij = Ricg,ij +τK Kij − 2(K 2 )ij − u−1 (LX K)ij + (Hess u)ij ,

Equation (8.23) with Λc = −3 gives Rg = −12. Equation (8.6) then gives

Where µ > 0, equality forces |X| = u, |J| = µ, and J = −µX ♭ /u. Where µ = 0, J = 0.
Substitution into all the normal, mixed and spatial components gives

                        Gg − 3g = 8πµu−2 ξ ♭ ⊗ ξ ♭ ,     µ(u2 − |X|2 ) = 0.

Since the matter term is tracefree, this is exactly (8.8). It allows nonzero matter on the null set;
its exclusion is a separate argument in the next section.

Outer area minimization gives H ≥ 0, by the first area variation for arbitrary nonnegative outward

with principal part −∆S . The strong minimum principle applies: after reversing the operator
and subtracting a sufficiently large zeroth-order constant, the usual nonpositive zeroth-order
condition holds because u ≥ 0. On connected S, either u > 0 everywhere or u = 0 everywhere.
This completes all assertions.

the treatment of an arbitrary interior null set.

Lemma 9.6 (Static equations and connectedness). Equations (9.6) hold on P , and P is connected.
Proof. Locally write A = df and T0 = t − f . Then g = h − λ2 dT02 . Equation (9.4) is vacuum on
P . The only mixed-time Christoffel symbols of this static product are
                                ΓTiT00 = λ−1 ∂i λ,           ΓiT0 T0 = λhij ∂j λ.
Thus its spatial Ricci tensor is Rich −λ−1 Hessh λ, and its time-time component is λ∆h λ. Com-
paring with −3g gives the first two equations in Equation (9.6); tracing gives Rh = −6. These
equations are intrinsic and hence hold globally on P .
    The end belongs to a single component of P . Any other component has compact closure in
the original manifold with boundary. The continuous function λ is zero on every finite boundary
point of that component, including a possible point on S. It therefore attains a positive maximum
at an interior point, contrary to ∆h λ = 3λ. There is no other component.

9.3   Optical completeness and simple connectivity
Lemma 9.7 (Completeness after truncation of the ordinary end). Equip P with the optical
metric k = λ−2 h. All escape directions other than the ordinary end have infinite k-length. After
truncating the ordinary end at a sufficiently large sphere, the resulting manifold with boundary is
complete for its intrinsic optical distance. The same is true of each of its Riemannian covering
spaces.
Proof. Near Z, smooth nonnegativity and compactness give N ≤ C distg (·, Z)2 . If ρ = distg (·, Z),
then | dρ|g ≤ 1 almost everywhere and
                           | d log ρ|2k = N C( d log ρ, d log ρ) ≤ N ρ−2 ≤ C.
Hence approach to Z has infinite optical length. In the zero boundary lapse case the same
calculation uses s and N = O(s2 ).
    For positive boundary lapse it is essential to allow arbitrary tangential motion. In the g-normal
collar decompose X = X ν ν + XT . Since | ds|g = 1, the exact inverse-metric formula is
                                                     !
                                        (X ν )2              N
                       | ds|2k = N                              N + |XT |2g = O(s2 ).
                                                                           
                                     1−                  =                                       (9.22)
                                         u2                  u2



                                                     122
Here N ≍ s, XT = O(s), and u is bounded below. Therefore | ds(v)| ≤ Cs|v|k for every vector
v, and any curve approaching S has infinite optical length. This proves the assertion without
restricting to normal curves.
    After truncation, N is bounded above, so k ≥ cg. Any optical Cauchy sequence is consequently
Cauchy for g and has a limit in the compact original region with the large sphere attached. The
logarithmic estimates exclude a limit in Z or in S. Every other limit is an ordinary smooth point
of the truncated metric, including its large-sphere boundary. This proves completeness. A smooth
manifold with boundary and its intrinsic Riemannian distance is a locally compact length space;
completeness makes it proper. For a covering metric completeness follows by projecting a Cauchy
sequence and then taking an evenly covered small neighborhood of its limit. Sufficiently short
connecting paths remain in that neighborhood, so the sequence eventually lies in a single sheet
and converges there.

                                  f (a) = 0,      f ′ (s) = 12 (q(s) − q).

Then f (b) = 0, so the endpoints are unchanged; the second variation of squared speed changes by
−2f ′ and becomes the constant q < 0. Uniform Taylor expansion on [a, b] now makes the varied
curve timelike for all sufficiently small nonzero ε. It remains future directed because Ṫ > 0 on
the original compact segment. Its endpoint contacts remain transverse and directed into and
out of L; away from small endpoint intervals the original curve lies a positive distance from the
boundary. Thus the variation remains in L and is the forbidden timelike competitor.

Proposition 9.9 (Simple connectivity of the timelike region). The connected manifold P is
simply connected.

Proof. Suppose its universal cover is nontrivial. The ordinary end is a product of a sphere and a
ray and is simply connected. Its inverse image therefore consists of at least two disjoint copies.
Truncate every copy at the same large radius R. Their boundary faces are compact spheres. By
Equation (9.2) and the formula for h, their mean curvatures for the normals pointing into the
removed tails satisfy                       √
                                           2 1 + R2
                                    Hh =              + o(1) > 0.
                                               R
The truncated cover is connected: attaching or removing a product tail cannot join different
components of its complement. By Lemma 9.7 its intrinsic optical metric is complete and proper.
    Fix one face. The union of all the other faces is a closed set, since it is the inverse image
of the truncation sphere with the fixed component removed: local finiteness in evenly covered
neighborhoods makes each component open as well as closed within that inverse image. The
remaining union is disjoint from the fixed compact face. Properness implies that the positive
distance between that face and the union is attained. A minimizing path has no intermediate
contact with a face: a later contact with the initial face or an earlier contact with any other face
would shorten the selected distance. To check its endpoint directions without assuming optical
convexity, let ρ be the inward k-distance to the initial face in its smooth normal collar, and
parametrize the path by unit optical speed from t = 0. The bound |dρ|k = 1 gives ρ(γ(t)) ≤ t;
replacing the initial segment by a normal collar segment gives the reverse inequality by minimality.
Hence ρ(γ(t)) = t and γ̇ = ∇k ρ near the endpoint. Apply the same argument backwards at the
final face. The path is therefore an interior geodesic with transverse orthogonal endpoint contacts.
The static equations lift to the cover, so Lemma 9.8 gives a contradiction.

9.5    Exclusion of flat boundary null leaves
Here a boundary null leaf is called flat when Hessg N = 0 along it, equivalently when a′′ (y0 ) = 0
in Equation (9.24). We exclude these leaves first; the remaining null leaves are then ruled out in
Proposition 9.13.
    We first give the elementary orbitwise construction needed at a possibly singular compact
invariant set. The construction produces an arc through each point separately and requires no
manifold structure on that set.

Lemma 9.12 (Flat boundary null leaves cannot occur). Let (M, g) be a smooth three-dimensional
Riemannian manifold and let N ∈ C ∞ (M ) be nonnegative, with compact zero set Z = {N = 0}.
Put P = {N > 0}, with boundary taken in M . Suppose a neighborhood U of Z carries a smooth
nonvanishing one-form α defining a cooriented foliation by two-dimensional leaves. Assume that
every sufficiently small foliated chart can be written as
                        α = B dy,        B > 0,      N = B a(y),           a ≥ 0,                (9.41)
with smooth coefficients. Let q be the√induced metric on a plaque Σ = {y = y0 } ⊂ ∂P , let KΣ be
its Gaussian curvature, and put b = B. Suppose the following leaf equations hold on every such
plaque:
                       HessΣ b = 12 b(KΣ + 3)q,    ∆Σ (b2 ) = 6b2 − a′′ (y0 ).             (9.42)
Then Hessg N (p) ̸= 0 for every p ∈ ∂P . Equivalently, every boundary zero in Equation (9.41)
satisfies a′′ (y0 ) > 0.

     Choose a neighborhood U0 of Z with compact closure in U and multiply Y by a smooth

{t : ϕt (p) ∈ F } is both open and closed in R for every p ∈ F . In all subsequent uses outside F we

   In a foliated chart at p, transversality gives dξ y(γp (ξ))|ξ=0 ̸= 0. After restriction, the transverse
coordinate of the arc covers an open interval containing y(p). Equation (9.41) and positivity of
B then imply that a vanishes on that interval. This gives an open neighborhood of p on which
N = 0, contradicting p ∈ ∂P . Hence F is empty. Finally a′′ ≥ 0 at any zero of the nonnegative
function a, so Equation (9.43) proves the stated strict positivity at every boundary zero.

Proposition 9.13 (No interior null set). One has Z = ∅, and hence P = M .

Proof. Apply Lemma 9.12 with α = X ♭ ; its hypotheses are precisely the compactness in Lemma 9.2
and the factorization and equations in Lemma 9.10. Every boundary zero therefore satisfies
a′′ > 0. It is therefore an isolated zero in the transverse coordinate, and its neighborhood consists

                                                    130
of a smooth two-sided zero leaf with P on both sides. There can be no open null region. Indeed, if
the interior of Z were nonempty, it would have a boundary in the connected manifold M , since P
is nonempty. Such a boundary point lies in ∂P and would have the just-described neighborhood
with no open zero region, a contradiction. Thus all of Z equals ∂P , and it is a compact embedded
hypersurface. The local isolation and compactness give only finitely many components. The
nonvanishing global form X ♭ coorients each of them.
    On a component of Z, the local positive functions B1 /a′′ patch to a global smooth function.
To check this, another oriented transverse coordinate has the form ye = ϕ(y) with ϕ′ > 0. At a
zero leaf,
                            e1 = B1 /ϕ′ ,
                            B                a = ϕ′ a,
                                             e             a′′ = a′′ /ϕ′ ,
                                                           e

where the last derivatives use ye and a = a′ = 0. The quotient is therefore invariant. Since a′′ is
constant along each plaque, Equation (9.25) gives

                                                  B1             B1
                                                       
                                        ∆qy                 =6       − 1.
                                                  a′′            a′′

The maximum and minimum principles on the compact component imply B1 /a′′ = 1/6. Its leaf
derivative is zero; thus b1 is constant along each plaque, and the first equation in Equation (9.25)
gives Ky = −3.
    Cut the original manifold along these finitely many two-sided components and truncate its
ordinary end. The resulting compact orientable three-manifold L is connected and has interior
homotopy equivalent to P : the end and each of the original and cut boundary collars are product
collars, which may be shortened without changing homotopy type. Proposition 9.9 therefore gives
H1 (L; Z) = H 1 (L; Z) = 0. Poincaré–Lefschetz duality and the boundary long exact sequence give

      H2 (L, ∂L; Z) ∼
                    = H 1 (L; Z) = 0,     0 = H2 (L, ∂L; Z) −→ H1 (∂L; Z) −→ H1 (L; Z) = 0.

Consequently every component of the orientable boundary is a sphere. In particular each cut
copy of a component of Z is a sphere, incompatible with its constant Gaussian curvature −3 by
Gauss–Bonnet. Thus Z must have been empty.

9.6    The regular boundary of the quotient

    If u|S > 0, Equation (9.11) makes N a smooth original defining function. In coordinates

    Since a0 − (2κh )−1 vanishes at N = 0, division by N also gives Equation (9.51). Its remainder

present. The remaining differential is smooth in the original collar. Fixing values on one interior
collar section and integrating the normal derivative to S gives a smooth extension; its tangential
derivatives agree with the prescribed differential by continuity. This proves Equation (9.53).

Proof of Theorem 9.1. Propositions 9.9 and 9.13 give P = M and its simple connectivity. Lem-
mas 9.5, 9.6, and 9.14 prove the stated closedness, equations, and boundary data.
    The
      √ quotient completion has the same underlying topology as Ω: in the positive-lapse case
N 7→ N changes the boundary smooth structure but is a homeomorphism of the closed collar,
and its restriction to S is the identity. To check completeness, let a path have finite h-length.
Since h ≥ g in the interior, it is a g-Cauchy path and has a limit in the complete original manifold
with boundary. An interior limit is an ordinary smooth quotient point because N > 0 there. A
limit on S is included by Lemma 9.14; in its smooth quotient collar the same path tends to that
boundary point. The unique ordinary end has infinite g-distance, and hence infinite h-distance.
These exhaust all possible finite-length escapes and prove completeness.
                                                      ′
    Finally, Equation (9.2) gives N = V02 (1 + O2 (r−τ )). Since spatial lowering by g preserves the
stated end orders,
                                                ′      √                    ′
                      N −1 X ♭ ⊗ X ♭ = O2 (r−2τ ),       N − V0 = O2 (r1−τ ).

These are Equation (9.8). In particular the attached boundary is compact, the quotient has the
single designated end, and no additional completion points have been introduced.

concern the original exterior, rather than an auxiliary deformed manifold.

10.1    Completion at the horizon and preservation of equality
Proposition 10.1 (The complete equality base). The metric h has a smooth completion by the
original boundary S, with its possibly changed transverse smooth coordinate. Its boundary data are

                     h|T S = g|T S ,     λ|S = 0,         ∂νh λ = κ,        IIh |S = 0.             (10.2)

The completed metric is complete, and S is outer area-minimizing for h. Its four metric fluxes
agree with those of g in the original balanced chart. In particular,

                                                                                    rh + rh3
                  Amin,h (S) = A0 ,      p(h) = (m, 0, 0, 0),           p0 (h) =             .      (10.3)
                                                                                       2
For some τ1 > 3/2, the end satisfies

           h − b = O2,α1 (r−τ1 ),      λ − V0 = O2,α1 (r1−τ1 ),           h − g = O2,α1 (r−2τ1 ).   (10.4)

The last estimate is in physical tensor norm.

Proof. We spell out the completion because the original boundary smooth structure is needed
again in the spacetime reconstruction. The boundary alternatives and first jets are supplied by
Theorem 8.1 and Lemma 9.14.
    Suppose first that u|S > 0. Then N is an original smooth defining function, positive into the
exterior, and
                                                  1
                                        X ♭ |S =    dN |S .
                                                 2κ
In coordinates (N, y) on a collar, smooth division gives
                                                                                     1
                      X ♭ = a(N, y) dN + N βA (N, y) dy A ,             a(0, y) =      .
                                                                                    2κ



                                                    133
Set N = λ2 . The coefficients of the base metric become
                                          hλλ = 4λ2 gN N + 4a2 ,
                                          hλA = 2λ(gN A + aβA ),                                (10.5)
                                                           2
                                       hAB = gAB + λ βA βB ,

where the right sides are evaluated at (λ2 , y). These coefficients extend smoothly through λ = 0,
with even diagonal blocks and an odd mixed block. Reflection in λ = 0 is an isometry. At
the boundary, hλλ = κ−2 and hλA = 0, proving the first three assertions in (10.2); its fixed
hypersurface is totally geodesic.
    If u|S = 0, let s be the original inward-to-domain normal coordinate. The preceding boundary
jets give
                          u = sa(s, y),     X = s2 Y (s, y),    a(0, y) = κ.
Consequently, with D = a2 − s2 |Y |2g ,
                                                               √
                       h = g + s2 D−1 Y ♭ ⊗ Y ♭ ,         λ = s D,    D|S = κ2 .
These are smooth in the original collar. The static Hessian equation extends continuously to S
and gives Hessh λ = 0 there. Its tangential part is κIIh , so the boundary is totally geodesic. In
h-Gaussian distance ρ it also gives
                        h = dρ2 + q(ρ),        q ′ (0) = 0,    λ = κρ + O(ρ3 ).                 (10.6)
    The completion has the same underlying topology as the original closed exterior. A finite-
length escaping h-curve is a finite-length g-curve because h ≥ g. Completeness of the original
metric space therefore gives a limit in the original closed exterior. Interior points are ordinary
smooth base points because N > 0 everywhere in the interior, and points of S have just been
attached by regular collars. The end has infinite h-length by its hyperbolic asymptotics. Thus
there is no missing finite-length escape.
    For an interior smooth enclosing cut, the quadratic-form inequality h ≥ g gives its h-area at
least its g-area, which is at least A0 . A cut smooth in the completed base and meeting S can be
approximated, in area, by cuts whose meeting portions have been moved a positive distance into
a collar. To see that the full-boundary convention is preserved, apply a smooth inward collar
map to the entire exterior domain defining the cut, taper it to the identity outside a fixed collar,
and take its full intrinsic boundary. In the positive-lapse case perform this construction in the
regular λ collar and keep the displacement positive before regarding the cut as an original smooth
interior cut. Collar smoothness makes the area error tend to zero. Hence every completed-base
cut has area at least A0 , while S itself has area A0 and remains an admissible full boundary.
    Choose
                                         3
                                           < τ1 < min(2, τdata )
                                         2
slightly below the exponent in the adjoint end estimates. Those estimates give u − V0 , X =
O2,α1 (r1−τ1 ). Since N ∼ r2 , the identity defining h gives the final estimate in (10.4). The identity
λ − u = −|X|2g /(u + λ) gives the estimate for λ; the estimate for h − b follows from that for g − b.
    Each metric-flux integrand is linear in the error and its first background derivative and has a
static-potential factor of size O(r). The difference contributed by h − g is therefore O(r3−2τ1 )
after integration over a coordinate sphere, and tends to zero. The same estimate holds for all four
potentials. The existing mass limits of g consequently give the stated limits for h. This proves
(10.3) and the proposition.

                                                    134
10.2    The Riemannian Einstein metric
Use a circle coordinate θ of any fixed positive period on the end and put

                                          G = h + λ2 dθ2 .                                     (10.7)

The warped-product Christoffel symbols involving the circle are Γθiθ = λi /λ and Γiθθ = −λ∇ih λ.
Thus

    RicG |T Ω = Rich −λ−1 Hessh λ = −3h,         (RicG )θθ = −λ∆h λ = −3λ2 ,     (RicG )iθ = 0.
                                                                                               (10.8)
In particular G is a four-dimensional Riemannian Einstein metric. It is invariant under circle
translation and time reflection θ 7→ −θ.
    Only an end collar is needed for the conformal-boundary regularity theorem below. The
following also explains why the horizon causes no singularity if a global Riemannian filling is used.
Lemma 10.2 (The regular circle bolt). If the period of θ is 2π/κ, collapsing its circles over S
extends (10.7) across a regular bolt. In polar Cartesian coordinates it is initially a C 2,α Einstein
metric for every 0 < α < 1 and hence admits smooth Einstein coordinates across the bolt.
Proof. Use (10.6) and set ϑ = κθ, of period 2π. On each boundary coordinate patch, write
x = ρ cos ϑ and y = ρ sin ϑ. The normal part is
                                                       (λ/(κρ))2 − 1
                  dρ2 + (λ/κ)2 dϑ2 = dx2 + dy 2 +                    (x dy − y dx)2 .
                                                            ρ2
The coefficient in front of the final squared one-form is a smooth one-sided function of ρ and the
bolt variables, equal to a smooth bolt function plus O(ρ). The squared one-form has quadratic
Cartesian coefficients. Its O(ρ) correction is consequently C 2,α for every α < 1. Likewise
q(ρ) = q(0) + ρ2 q2 + O(ρ3 ) gives a C 2,α tangential block. There are no mixed dρ–bolt terms in
Gaussian coordinates. Positivity follows at ρ = 0 from q(0) > 0.
    The Einstein equation holds away from the bolt and, because the metric is C 2 , also holds at the
bolt by continuity of Ricci curvature. Interior Einstein regularity in harmonic coordinates applies,
for instance [12, Theorem 5.2]. This gives smooth, indeed analytic, Einstein coordinates there.
The circle action and its fixed set are then smooth: isometries preserve harmonic coordinates and
the local Killing field satisfies the corresponding elliptic equation. Normal exponential coordinates
to the fixed set recover the usual smooth disk action. Thus this is a regular bolt, with no conical
angle defect.

10.3    A weighted gauge on the hyperbolic tube
The background for the end calculation is the complete hyperbolic tube

                  G0 = b + V02 dθ2 = dη 2 + sinh2 η σ + cosh2 η dθ2     on B 3 × S 1 .         (10.9)

Its sectional curvature is −1. Near infinity the defining function z0 = 2e−η gives
                                   h                                        i
                        G0 = z0−2 dz02 + (1 − z02 /4)2 σ + (1 + z02 /4)2 dθ2 .               (10.10)

We extend z0 to a positive smooth function in the interior when defining global weighted spaces.
All such extensions give equivalent norms. These are the intrinsic weighted Hölder norms measured
in bounded physical coordinate patches; a tensor in Cδk,α has physical size and derivatives O(z0δ ).

                                                 135
Lemma 10.3 (Gauge and improved decay). Let an Einstein metric G on a tube end satisfy
G − G0 ∈ Cβ2,α for some β > 3/2. After a boundary-identity change of coordinates, its difference
from G0 belongs to Cδk,α0 on a smaller end for every integer k and every 0 < δ < 3. Here one
can choose α0 > 0 below the original exponent. The coordinate change preserves circle translation
and time reflection if G does. In particular G has an ordinary C 2,α2 conformal compactification
for some α2 > 0.

Proof. Fix γ with 3/2 < γ < min(2, β) and lower the Hölder exponent slightly. First cut G − G0

moved out. The cutoff is a function of η, so retains the stated symmetries.

background. For ∆L + 6 in dimension four the indicial radius is 3/2, so P is an isomorphism at
every physical weight 0 < δ < 3. Cut k off once more toward the interior. Its equation on the
whole tube is (10.13) plus a smooth compactly supported commutator. Since 2γ > 3, that source
belongs to Cδj,α0 for every δ < 3. Solve it at any δ with 2 < δ < 3 and compare with the cut-off
k at weight γ. Their difference is in the kernel at the smaller weight, hence vanishes. Higher
weighted regularity follows by the same equation. Smaller positive weights follow by inclusion.
    Finally, for an ordinary compact coordinate component of z02 k, two coordinate derivatives have
size O(z0δ−2 ). The physical Hölder estimate, compared on patches of compact diameter comparable
to z0 , gives an ordinary C 2,α2 extension whenever 0 < α2 < min(α0 , δ − 2). Equivalently this is
the weighted-to-ordinary inclusion of [18, Lemma 3.7(b)], with the tensor weight included. This
proves the claimed preliminary conformal compactness.

Proof. Equations (10.4) and (10.7) give G − G0 ∈ Cτ2,α 1
                                                          1 in physical four-dimensional tensor norm:

                                     2    2    2     −τ
the relative circle coefficient is (λ − V0 )/V0 = O(r ). Apply Lemma 10.3. The resulting metric
                                                        1

is smooth in the interior, Einstein with Ric = −3G, has an ordinary C 2 conformal compactification,

    These are the hypotheses of [9, Theorem A]. That theorem is local to a collar of a compact
conformal boundary and does not require a global bolt filling. It supplies a boundary-identity
collar diffeomorphism placing the metric in geodesic form. Its compactification is smooth in
even total dimension, hence in the present dimension four. The defining function is chosen for
the specified representative. Both its defining eikonal equation and the inward normal flow are
invariant under circle translation and reflection; uniqueness with that boundary normalization
makes the collar map equivariant. Equivariance under translations permits at most a spatially
dependent circle shift, and reflection together with boundary identity forces that shift to vanish.
Thus the geodesic gauge is a spatial one and has the block form in (10.14).
    For completeness we compute the coefficients needed later. Write

normalization gives zo λ → 1, while (10.14) gives zλ → 1. The collar changes preserve the same
unrescaled lapse, hence z/zo → 1 at corresponding points. The harmonic-map displacement gives
z0 /zo → 1 in the intermediate gauge, so also z/z0 → 1.

    The expansion fixes the decay and normalization needed to evaluate the static Heintze–Karcher
deficit in the next section. Its third-order trace records the mass, while the horizon area and
surface gravity supply the inner boundary term.


11     Rigidity of the normalized static base
The equality data determine the complete static base. We use a static Heintze–Karcher deficit
on smooth bounded domains, followed by an exhaustion and the finite-domain warped-product
rigidity of [4, Theorem 1.1].

                                                            b    Every critical point would be an
isolated nondegenerate maximum. Fix a high regular level in the horizon collar. Each compact
band between it and a positive lower regular level has finitely many critical points. On decreasing
the level through a nondegenerate maximum the superlevel set acquires a disjoint ball; between
critical levels the normalized gradient flow preserves its components. No such components can
merge without a different kind of critical point. A component created at a maximum could thus
never join the horizon component at a positive lower level. This contradicts a path from that
maximum to the horizon collar, whose positive continuous function φ has a positive minimum.
This proves the assertion. Normalized gradient flow on compact bands now gives the global
product by connected spherical levels of φ.

12.1    The interior graph and its future normal
Lemma 12.1 (The original interior data). There is a smooth function H on int Ω for which
                                                                      

Proof. By Theorem 9.1, the closed one-form A = N −1 X ♭ has a global primitive with dH = −A.
Set T = t + H(x) on R × int Ω. Completion of the square gives the exact identity

                      h − N (dT )2 = −u2 dt2 + gij (dxi + X i dt)(dxj + X j dt).               (12.3)

particular,
                      I ∗ gM = h − N dH ⊗ dH = h − N −1 X ♭ ⊗ X ♭ = g.
The projection of the graph to the static base is the diffeomorphism Ψ, so the graph is injective
and is an embedding in the interior.
   Choose the time orientation for which ∂T = ∂t is future timelike in this exterior. The unit

                          n = u−1 (∂t − X),       gM (n, ∂t ) = −u < 0,

for the original tensor. Hence the induced future second form agrees with that original tensor.

Lemma 12.2 (Smoothness in the original boundary structure). The graph (12.2) extends smoothly
to the original closed manifold Ω. If u|S > 0, its boundary is a full smooth cross-section of U = 0,
V > 0. If u|S = 0, its boundary is the bifurcation sphere. In both cases the boundary map is a
diffeomorphism onto that section.
Proof. First suppose u|S > 0. By Lemma 9.14, N is a smooth defining function in the original
collar and
                                 1                           1
                           A=       d log N + β,    H = − log N + B,                           (12.7)
                                2κ                          2κ
where β is smooth and closed up to S and B is smooth there. To justify the last assertion directly,
the differential of the displayed remainder in H is −β. Integrating its bounded normal derivative
gives a boundary value; tangential derivatives then extend by the smooth differential equation.
This works to every order and on overlapping collar patches.
    The angular coordinates of Ψ are also smooth
                                              √     in the original collar. Here is the point that is
needed because the regular base uses λ = N . Formula (10.5) doubles h across λ = 0 by the
isometry (λ, y) 7→ (−λ, y). The angular coordinate ω of the model is its normal footpoint on
the horizon: in the model, curves of constant ω are the normal geodesics from the boundary. A
boundary-preserving isometry has the same description on the base. Reflection takes each such
geodesic to its continuation with the same footpoint. Therefore ω is a smooth even function
of λ in doubled collar coordinates. A smooth even function of one real variable, with smooth
parameters, is a smooth function of its square on the half-line. This follows, to each finite order,
from Taylor expansion with its smooth remainder. Thus ω = ω(N, y) is smooth in the original
collar. Likewise r = F −1 (N ) is smooth.
    Substituting (12.7) into (12.4)–(12.5) gives on the graph

                              U = −Q(N ) N e−κB ,       V = Q(N )eκB .                         (12.8)

These are smooth original collar functions. On S they satisfy U = 0 and V = Q(0)eκB > 0. The
restriction ω : S → S 2 is a diffeomorphism because Ψ extends as a boundary isometry. Hence the
image is the graph of a smooth positive function V (ω) over the whole horizon sphere, meeting
each generator once. The horizon first form is rh2 σ, so this section is spacelike.
    Now suppose u|S = 0. In the original normal coordinate s ≥ 0, Lemma 9.14 gives smooth
fields

       u = sa,    X = s2 Y,    N = s2 D,   D = a2 − s2 |Y |2g ,   D|S = κ2 ,   A = D−1 Y ♭ .

                              U = −Q(N ) λe−κH ,       V = Q(N ) λeκH .                        (12.9)

                                                145
They extend smoothly with U = V = 0 on S and with nonzero normal derivatives of opposite
signs. The angular map again gives a diffeomorphism of S onto the entire bifurcation sphere.
    In either case the extended map has induced metric g at S by continuity of the identity in
Lemma 12.1, since all ambient and map coefficients just displayed are smooth. For any nonzero
tangent vector v of Ω, including at its boundary,

                                 gM (dI(v), dI(v)) = g(v, v) > 0.

Thus dI is injective and its image is spacelike up to the boundary. This establishes smoothness
and immersion in the original, rather than merely the completed-base, structure.

Proposition 12.3 (Properness, the future second form, and infinity). The extended map I is a
smooth proper spacelike embedding of the original (Ω, g, K) into the smooth maximal Schwarzschild–
AdS extension of parameter M = mAH . Its interior lies in one exterior, its boundary is the full
future horizon section described in Lemma 12.2, and its end reaches that exterior’s conformal
infinity.

Proof. The interior map is injective by its base projection, the boundary map is injective by
Lemma 12.2, and their images are disjoint since r > rh in the interior and r = rh on S. Let C be
a compact subset of the maximal spacetime. Its radius function is bounded above, say by R. The
preimage I −1 (C) is closed and lies in

                                        Ψ−1 [rh , R] × S 2 ,
                                                          


which is compact in the completed base. That completion is homeomorphic to the original closed
exterior, so it is also compact in Ω. Hence I is proper. A proper injective immersion is an
embedding, including for manifolds with boundary.
    The future unit normal extends smoothly to S. One can construct it in any of the regular
charts by choosing a smooth future timelike ambient vector, projecting it onto the normal line of
the smooth spacelike tangent bundle, and normalizing. These local choices give the unique future
normal and therefore agree on overlaps. In the interior it is the normal used in Lemma 12.1. Its
second form is smooth up to S and agrees with the original K in the interior; continuity proves
equality at S as well.
    For the end statement, temporarily denote the original end radius by ro . The original estimates
give
                                    |X|g
                             |A|g =      = O(ro−1−τ1 ),      τ1 > 3/2.
                                     N
Consequently, in the original radial and angular coordinates,

                              Aro = O(ro−2−τ1 ),      AA = O(ro−τ1 ).

tends to zero
         p    by the second bound, so this limit is a single constant T∞ . Moreover λ/  1 + ro2 → 1
and λ = F (r), which imply r/ro → 1. The unique end therefore has r → ∞ and T → T∞ . The
base isometry covers a full model end, so all angles occur. In the standard conformal completion
with defining function 1/r, the graph thus reaches the full section T = T∞ of this exterior’s
conformal infinity. All these conclusions follow from the reconstructed graph and its specific
decay estimates.

Proposition 12.4 (Converse on the stated horizon subclass). Suppose the original data satisfy
the connected horizon hypotheses of Definition 2.6 and have the embedding required in Theorem 1.2,
with its stipulated actual metric mass M = mAH . Then equality holds in the Penrose Inequality.

Proof. In a regular horizon chart the first form of U = 0 has null generator ∂V in its kernel and
angular part rh2 σ. Every full smooth spacelike cross-section therefore has area 4πrh2 , including the
bifurcation section. The embedding and the assumed outer area-minimization give

                                     Amin = Areag (S) = 4πrh2 .

The equation FM (rh ) = 0 and the actual mass match now yield

                                       2                16π      4π
This uses the mass match as specified in the statement. No invariance under an arbitrary change
of asymptotic time slice is needed for this implication.

   Propositions 12.3 and 12.4 prove Theorem 1.2. The following examples also verify sharpness
without a maximality assumption.

Proposition 12.5 (Mass-matched static and nonstatic examples). For every M > 0, the static
exterior with its bifurcation boundary attains equality. It also admits smooth compactly supported
time graph perturbations with K ̸≡ 0 which satisfy all the initial-data and connected horizon
hypotheses and still attain equality with actual mass mAH = M .

Proof. Fix a closed annulus rh < a < b < ∞ and a smooth function φ supported in its interior.
On the graph T = φ(r, ω) the induced metric is

                                        qφ = q − F dφ ⊗ dφ.                                   (12.10)

For sufficiently small C 2 norm of φ it is positive and uniformly comparable with q, so its completion
is a smooth complete exterior with the same boundary. In proper distance from the horizon, q is
smooth and has a totally geodesic boundary; φ = 0 on that collar. The graph lies in the vacuum
metric RicgM = −3gM , so its data satisfy the vacuum constraints and hence the dominant energy
condition. Near the boundary and near infinity its future second form vanishes and its metric is
exactly q.
    Every coordinate sphere with r > rh has
                                                     √
                                                    2 F

                                                     r
Its future expansion for the perturbed graph remains positive on [a, b] by C 2 smallness, uniformly
on this fixed annulus, and is unchanged outside it. If a weakly future trapped enclosing surface had
a component strictly outside S, take its maximum radius. There it touches a coordinate sphere
from the smaller radius side, with the same outward normal toward the end. The mean curvature
comparison gives Hsurface ≥ Hsphere , while the tangential planes and hence the tangential traces of
K agree. Its future expansion would be positive at that point, a contradiction. Thus the horizon
is outermost for the required future condition.
